SESSION NOVEMBER, 1918-JUNE, 1919. 
Thursday, November 14th, 1918. 
ANNUAL GENERAL MEETING. 


Prof. H. M. MACDONALD, President, in the Chair. 


Present twenty-four members and three visitors. 

Prof. Takeo Wada was elected a member of the Society. 

- The President announced the deaths of the following members of the 
Society :—Prof. U. Dini (Hon. For. Member), Prof. 0. Henrici (formerly 
President), Prof. B. Hopkinson, Prof. J. H. Woolston, and Mr. J. W. 
Sharpe. 

- The Treasurer presented his Report, which was duly received. 

The meeting proceeded to the election of a Council and Officers; the 
list is as follows :—President, J. E. Campbell ; Vice-Presidents, H. Hilton, 
E. W. Hobson, H. M. Macdonald; Treasurer, A. EK. Western ; Secretaries, 
T. J. VA. Bromwich, G. H. Hardy; other members of the Council, 
S. Chapman, A. L. Dixon, Miss H. P. Hudson, Sir J. Larmor, J. E. 
Littlewood, A. E. H. Love, Major P. A. MacMahon, J. W. Nicholson, 
G. N. Watson. 

Prof. H. M. Macdonald, having called Mr. Campbell to the Chair, gave 
his Presidential Address ; it was agreed that the Address should be printed 
in the Proceedings. 

Prof. M. J. M. Hill read a paper “On the Use of a Property of 
Jacobians to Determine the Character of any Solution of an Ordinary 
Differential Equation of the First Order, or of a Linear Partial Differential 
Equation of the First Order.” 

Mr. L. J. Mordell made an informal communication on the expression 
of a number as the sum of an odd number of squares.

In the absence of the authors, the following papers were communicated 
by title from the Chair :— 


*On the Roots of a certain Equation in Spherical Harmonics: Prof. 
H. J. Priestley. 
*A Detail in Conformal Representation: Mr. J. Hodgkinson. 
On the Product of Semiconvergent Series: Mr. T. S. Broderick. 
*A Simple Condition for Co-apolar Triangles: Dr. W. P. Milne. 


ABSTRACT. 
A Statement by Fermas 


Mr. L. J. Morpetu. 


It is well known that nearly three hundred years ago Fermat, in 
stating what is now known as Fermat’s last theorem, namely that, if » 
be an integer greater than 2, the equation «”+y” = z” does not admit of 
integer solutions apart from the trivial case when one of the unknowns is 
zero, asserted that he had discovered a truly wonderful solution, but that 
the margin of his book was too small to contain it. 

No general proof has been found by mathematicians, even though nine 
years ago a prize of about £5000 was established for the first proof. 
Hence the question has been discussed whether Fermat was in possession 
of a proof. From what is known of his character, it seems certain that, at 
any rate, he was under the impression that he had a valid proof. It is 
therefore of interest to analyse other statements made by him. 

He had proposed as a problem to the English mathematicians to show 
that the equation «+2= vy? has only one integer solution, evidently 
given by « = 5, y= 3. On this he has a note to the effect that he had 
discovered an entirely new method—sane pulcherrima et subtilissima— 
which enabled him to solve such questions in integers.t 

I have noticed recently that no equation of the form z*+a=y’ can 
have more than a finite number of solutions in integers. For, in a paper 
“Indeterminate Equations of the Third and Fourth Degrees,” in the 
Quarterly Journal of Mathematics for 1914, I have shown that all the 
integer solutions can be found by solving in integers a finite number of 


* Printed in this volume. 
T Cf. Ball’s Mathematical Recreations, p. 43. 
b 2 


vi RECORDS UF PROCEEDINGS AT MEETINGS. 


equations of the form f(z, y) = 6, where f(z, y) is a homogeneous binary 
quartic in z, y. It follows therefore, as a particular case of a theorem of 
Thue in Crelle’s Journal for 1909, that such an equation can have only a 
finite number of integer solutions [except when f(z, y) is a perfect square] ; 
and hence the same holds for the equation z?+a = y’. 

It seems fairly certain that this result was not known to Fermat, and 
hence his method must have been such as to give him only a few integer 
solutions. Perhaps it may have been equivalent to some such process as 
follows: in #?+2 = 4° put y = a@’+20°, and take 


ttJf—2= (atbr/—2). 


By equating imaginary parts, 


‘ 


1 = 3a2b —20? = b (8a2—20%), 


whence @a=1, b= 1, so that y= 38, x = 5. In any case, it appears 
doubtful if Fermat’s description of his method would apply at the present 
time. 


Thursday, December 12th, 1918. 
Mr. J. E. CAMPBELL, President, in the Chair. 


Present eleven members and three visitors. 

Mr. H. W. Turnbull, Capt. J. McPolin, and Mr. 8S. R. U. Savoor, were 
elected members of the Society. 

The Auditor (Lt.-Col. Cunningham) presented his report: on the 
motion of Mr. Mordell, seconded by Dr. Airey, the Treasurer’s report was 
adopted, and the thanks of the Society were voted to the Treasurer and to 
the Auditor. 

Mr. G. H. Hardy communicated a paper (written in conjunction with 
Mr. J. E. Littlewood) “ Applications of the Method of Farey Dissection in 
the Analytic Theory of Numbers:—(i) A new solution of Waring’s 
Problem ; (ii) Proof that every large number is the sum of at most 38 
biquadrates ; (iii) The Riemann Hypothesis and the expression of a num- 
ber as the sum of a stated number of primes”. Remarks on the com- 
munication were made by Major MacMahon and Mr. Mordell. 

Mr. B. M. Wilson communicated a paper (written in conjunction with 
Mr. N. M. Shah) “‘ Numerical Data connected with Goldbach’s Theorem”. 


RECORDS OF PROCEEDINGS AT MEETINGS. vil 


Lieut. M. Fréchet made an informal communication “ Integrals in 
Abstract Fields’’.* 


ABSTRACTS. 


Applications of the Method of Farey Dissection in the Analytic 
Theory of Numbers 


Mr. G. H. Harpy and Mr. J. E. Lirrnewoop. 


It was first proved by Hilbert (Math. Annalen, Vol. 67, 1909, 
pp. 281-805) that, as was asserted over a hundred years earlier by 
Waring (Meditationes Algebraicae, ed. 8, Cambridge, 1782, pp. 849-350), 
every number 7 is the sum of at most g(k) positive k-th powers, g(k) 
being a function of & alone. In this paper a new proof of this theorem 
is given, based on entirely different ideas drawn from the theory of 
analytic functions. 

It is also shown that every large number [that is to say every number 
greater than a certain number 7(k), depending on k alone] is the sum of 


at most bk. Qh34-41 


positive k-th powers. In particular every large number is the sum of at 
most 33 fourth, or 198 sixth, powers. 

The method used is also applied to other problems. It is shown, for 
example, that, if we assume the truth of Riemann’s hypothesis concerning 
the roots of the Zeta-function, and of a generalisation of this hypothesis 
to the whole body of similar functions which occur in the theory of 
primes, then every large number is the sum of at most five primes. 

In all the applications of the method far more is proved than a bare 
existence theorem: actual asymptotic formule are found for the numbers 
of representations. 

A somewhat fuller account of the nature of these researches (in so far 
as they are concerned with Waring’s problem) will be published shortly in 
the Quarterly Journal of Mathematics. Reference should also be made 
to two short notes, by Messrs. N. M. Shah and B. M. Wilson, and by 
Messrs. Hardy and Littlewood, which are about to appear in the Proceed- 
ings of the Cambridge Philosophical Society. These notes deal with the 


* The substance of this communication has appeared in the Bulletin de la Société Mathe- 
matique de France. 


Vili RECORDS OF PROCEEDINGS AT MEETINGS. 


application of the method to ‘‘ Goldbach’s Theorem ” (that every number 
is the sum of two primes) and other unproved theorems of the same 
character. Here no rigorous demonstration is offered, even on the basis 
of an unproved hypothesis; but the method is shown to lead to very=re- 


markable formule, which stand the test of numerical comparison with 
the facts.

ABSTRACT. 


General Pentaspherical Coordinates 
Mr. T. C. Lewis. 


1. The ratios of the power to radius for a finite point with regard to 
any five spheres (whose centres are non-cospherical, and no four co- 
planar) are the pentaspherical coordinates of the point. If the spheres 
form an orthogonal system, the five centres must be such that each 
is the orthocentre of a tetrahedron of which the remaining four are the 
vertices. If the five centres be not so related, it is still known that 
the sum of the reciprocals of the powers of each point with regard to 
the circumsphere of the other four is zero. Take as spheres of reference 
spheres with radii p,, ..., ps5, such that 8p} is equal to the power of the 
first point with regard to the circumsphere of the other four, and similarly 
for p3, &c. 

Then whether the system be orthogonal or not the following two rela- 
tions hold, viz.,

On the Solution of the Quintic 
Mr. J. Hammonp. 


I can form a couple of quintics (or rather three such couples, though 
there is generally no need to consider more than one of them), viz.,

Congruence Properties of Partitions 
Mr. 8S. Ramanugan. 


In a paper published recently in the Proceedings of the Cambridge 
Philosophical Society,* I proved a number of arithmetical properties of 
p(n), the number of unrestricted partitions of , and in particular that 


p(5n+4) =0 (mod 5), 
and p(7in+5) =0 (mod 7). 


Alternative proofs of these two theorems were found afterwards by Mr. 
H. B. C. Darling.t 

I have since found another method which enables me to prove all 
these properties and a variety of others, of which the most striking is 


p(lin+6) =0O (mod 11). 
There are also corresponding properties in which the moduli are powers 
Of 5, 7, or 11; thus 9(25n-+24) = 0 »(mod 25), 
p(49n+19), p(49n+88), p(49n+40), p(49n+47) = 0 (mod 49), 
p(121n+116)=0 (mod 121). 


It appears that there are no equally simple properties for any moduli in- 
volving primes other than these three. 
The function r(x) defined by the equation

On a Formula for an Area 
Prof. W. H. Youne. 


Although considerable progress has been made in recent years in the 
theory of the area of plane curves, the work done suffers from two defects : 
(1) the fact has been entirely overlooked, that area must, like length, be 
regarded ag possessing a sign, and that, in particular, even when the word 
area is used in the ordinary sense, as applied to a portion of the plane 
enclosed by a simple Jordan curve, it may be convenient to determine it 
as the algebraic sum, or the limit of the algebraic sum, of a number of 
positive and negative areas; (2) no satisfactory discussion has been given 
of the expression of an area as a double integral, even when the area is 
enclosed by a simple Jordan curve, and traced out by a doubly infinite 
series of simple Jordan curves, in such a manner as to set up a (1, 1)- 
correspondence = x (u, v), y =y (uw, v) between the area in the (2, y)- 
plane and the portion of the auxiliary (w, v)-plane of which it is the image. 

In the present paper, the author supplies what is lacking in both these 
respects. The results obtained are of fundamental importance alike in 
the theory of the area of surfaces, including the generalisations to which 


XX1V RECORDS OF PROCEEDINGS AT MEETINGS. 


this theory gives rise, and that of the transformation of the variables in 
a multiple integral. The author.has, in fact, been enabled to base on 
them an entirely new theory of the area of surfaces, which appears to be 
free from the very serious objections that have been made to earlier 
theories. Moreover, the main result of the paper, when interpreted in 
the light of a general theorem, proved by the author elsewhere, leads to 
conditions for the validity of the transformation of the variables ina 
multiple integral, of such generality as to render it probable that no 
appreciably wider conditions are obtainable. 

A definition is first given of the area of a polygonal figure, which 
may cross itself any number of times. This area F is defined to be half 
the resultant couple of a system of forces, represented in magnitude, line 
of action and sense, by the sides of the figure in order. The area A of a 
closed curve, c=2(u), y= yw), is then defined as the unique limit 
when this exists of the area F’ of a polygonal figure inscribed in it, the 
vertices of the figure following one another in the order determined by 
the increase of the parameter w, as the sides of the figure decrease in- 
definitely in length. 

With this definition it is shown that the formula

ABSTRACTS. 


The Zeros of Iommel’s Polynomials 


Dr. G. N. Watson. 


The zeros of Bessel functions of negative order have been investigated 
by Hurwitz (Math. Ann., Vol. 33), who treated the functions as limiting 
forms of Lommel’s polynomials. The object of the present communica- 
tion is to point out and rectify some slight defects in the analysis given 
by Hurwitz. The results obtained by him are sv important in the theory 
of Bessel functions that the proof of them should be made as rigorous as 
possible.

ABSTRACTS. 


Groups imvolving Three and only Three Operators which are 
Square 


Prof. G. A. MinuEr. 


In 1854 A. Cayley inaugurated abstract definitions of groups by 
defining abstractly the groups of order 8. About two years later W. R. 
Hamilton gave abstract definitions of the groups which are simply iso- 
morphic with the groups of movements of the five ancient regular solids. 
Since this time various other categories of groups have been defined 
abstractly, including those non-Abelian groups which R. Dedekind defined, 
in 1897, by the property that every sub-group is invariant, and known as 
Hamiltonian groups. In the present paper the author defines an addi- 
tional category of groups by the property that three and only three of 
their operators are squares of operators of the group. He proves that 
there are eight infinite systems of such groups. Seven of these systems 
are similar to the Hamiltonian groups as regards the property that there 
is one and only one smallest non-Abelian group in the system, and all the 
other groups of the system are direct products having for one factor this 
smallest group, while the other factor is an Abelian group. The remain- 
ing system is composed of Abelian groups. 


* Printed in this volume 


RECORDS OF PROCEEDINGS AT MEETINGS. XX1X 


The significance of Apolar Triangles in Elliptic Function Theory 
Prof. W. P. Miuns, M.A., D.Sc., and D. G. Taytor, M.A., D.Sc. 


If S be a cubic curve and A its Hessian, the condition that the three 
points on S whose elliptic parameters are a, 8, y, shall constitute a triad 
apolar to the curve AS+H = 0 is 


AS apy t+ Hog, a 0, 


where = Say = 120(a) 9(8) P(y)—29' (8) @ (y)—9.2(a)—393, 
Hapy = = (a) (CB) 9' (y)—92= (6) Ply) —893= 9 (a) 493. 


It is found that the expression AS,s,+ Hug, can be resolved into two 
factors A, A, where A may be called the “ collinearity-factor’”’ and A the 
“ apolarity-factor”’. A is a function of the differences of the parameters 
B—y, y—a, a—f, and is therefore unaltered if for a, B, y we substitute 
—(a+7), —(6-+7), —(y+7). On the other hand, A is not independent 
of 7 if we make the above substitutions. We therefore have the following 
geometrical result :— 


If three points, which are not collinear, te on a cubic curve and are 
apolar to a given member of the syzygetic pencil of cubics through the 
flexes, then the three points obtained by projecting the three given points 
through a point of the cubic curve on to the curve again are still apolar 
to the same member of the syzygetie pencil. 


Some considerations on the General Theory of Ruled Surfaces 
C. V. Hanumanta Rao. 


The structure of a ruled surface situated in ordinary space is not at 
all simple. On the other hand, the structure of a scroll situated in its 
normal space is extremely simple; and the present paper is devoted to 
the study of such scrolls. The normal space of aseroll with two directrices 
is determined in terms of the characteristic numbers of its plane sections. 
This question is precisely the same as the question of the freedom of the 
complete system of curves determined by the system of plane sections, 
and leads to a discussion of the deficiency in freedom of the characteristic 


series. 


XXX ]XECORDS OF PROCEEDINGS AT MEETINGS. 


The Vibrations and Stability of a Rotating Cylinder 
Mr. F. B. Prppvcx. 


In pursuance of a note inserted in Proceedings, Ser. 2, Vol. 16 (1918), 
p. lviii, a revision is made of a paper in Proceedings, Ser. 2, Vol. 9 (1910), 
p. 352, involving serious errors. Southwell’s method of investigating elastic 
stability, by no means restricted to statical problems, is here used to find 
the period equation to the order of approximation usual in the theory of 
thin rods. If Q is the whirling speed for an assigned wave-length and w 
the angular velocity, the frequency of vibration about the state of steady 
motion for the same wave-length is given by the simple equation 
p =w+Q, and the character of the vibration is also simple, including 
whirling as a special case.

OBITUARY NOTICES 


PROFESSOR B. HOPKINSON.* 


Bertram Hopxinson came up to Trinity College from St. Paul’s School 
in 1892, having been elected’ to a major scholarship at a very early age. 
His reputation had preceded him: and much was expected of him on the 
ground of heredity. For he came of a family famous in Cambridge 
annals. His father John Hopkinson had been Senior Wrangler in 1871 : 
he then plunged immediately into the career in practical engineering 
which was suited to the combination of scientific and practical interests 
for which he was remarkable. His opportunity arrived when the trans- 
formation of Mechanical Engineering to an electrical basis rather suddenly 
became imminent: in the early stages of that great development he and 
Lord Kelvin above all others were the chief intellectual guiding forces : 
and the guidance of the evolution of the modern dynamo and electric 
motor was Dr. Hopkinson’s main share in the advance. Under such con- 
ditions it was a great advantage to his son to reside chiefly at home, and 
to have the run of the private laboratory in which fundamental investiga- 
tions in Magnetism were going on as a sort of by-play to his father’s 
strenuous engineering work. Later when a fully equipped laboratory at 
King’s College, London, was at the disposal of his father as honorary 
professor, the son’s name had begun to appear as joint author before he 
came up to the University. The relations with his father are brought out 
with unconscious grace in the biographical meraoir prefixed to the edition, 
prepared by him in two volumes, of his father’s Scventific Papers. 

With such antecedents it was natural that his first aim should be the 
Mathematical Tripos. Those who had to do with him in that connexion 
may remember a certain modest and deferential robustness and indepen- 
dence: he had obviously the gift of friendship with his peers: and it 
could not be said that he sank the manifold interests of University life in 
one intellectual ambition. He rowed in the Trinity boat at Henley _ Ill- 
ness compelled him to pass out with an aegrotat degree: but in the follow- 


* Reprinted by permission from the Cambridge Review, Oct. 1918. 


XXX1V OBITUARY NOTICES 


ing year (1896) full amends came in a position in the first division of the 
first class in the advanced mathematical subjects. 

In 1898 he contributed to the Proceedings of the London Mathematical 
Society* an academic dissertation ‘On Discontinuous Fluid Motion in- 
volving Sources and Vortices,”’ which will form pp. 1-21 of his Scientzfic 
Papers. 

But with the practical instincts of his family he required a wider field 
of activity than could come to a junior resident at the University. He 
accordingly began to prepare for the Bar, doubtless with a view to a 
scientific practice in questions of patents, for which his father’s con- 
nexions formed the best possible introduction. The experienced leader in 
whose chambers he worked has described him as the most brilliant pupil 
he ever had. An eminent judge with whom he acted recently as scientific 
assessor warmly appreciated his services and prophesied for him a brilliant 
future. 

He was pursuing a legal career with rising prospects when the loss of 
his father and a younger brother and two sisters in an Alpine accident in 
1898 changed the course of his life. His father’s practice, of most inter- 
esting and important type, as a consulting engineer, lay vacant: and with 
his usual unflinching courage the son resolved to abandon the Bar and 
take it up. With the aid in partnership of an uncle and one of his 
father’s former assistants he had carried it on with success for a few years 
when a new opening arose. 

The chance came to him in 1903 to return to Cambridge in a respon- 
sible position. On the departure of Sir Alfred Ewing to take up the office 
of Director of Education in the Royal Navy, the problem of filling the 
vacancy in the chair of Engineering was more difficult even than is usual 
in such cases, where a man of scientific eminence has to be enticed from 
the activities of a lucrative profession. The electors decided to offer the 
chair to Bertram Hopkinson. Their choice excited some surprise on 
account of his youth: but those who had been in contact with him on 
scientific or professional matters were found to have no misgivings. 

When he returned to Cambridge to direct a department then in the 
full tide of development, in which some of the staff were his seniors, there 
were serious problems to be faced. For instance, the success of the school 
had made it very popular, and the question of maintaining the standard 
at the cost possibly of depletion of the numbers was urgent. The new 
professor had no hesitation on this subject. A first class in Engineering 
has always been a select degree: and in time a preliminary qualifying 


* Proc. London Math. Soc., Ser. 1, Vol. 29 (1898). 


Brerrram Hopxtnson. XXKV 


examination was instituted to keep away from the Honours school stu- 
dents who were not qualified to profit by the course. He was moreover 
not likely to undervalue the importance of a broad and solid foundation in 
mathematical physics, for which a University course is so well adapted. 

He knew how to separate the general direction of his School from 
absorption in details: accordingly he had time to become influential in 
the work of Engineering science outside, and he promoted the progress of 
the science on many committees and organizations. In this way his own 
scientific investigations were kept in touch with the practical needs of 
professional life, and problems of importance came to his laboratory for 
his pupils to work on. Thus the rapid development of oil engines opened 
out many problems in the regulation of gaseous explosions: and the 
variety and special properties of the new kinds of steel, which have in- 
troduced so many possibilities, demanded precise investigations. In 
another domain, problems on impact led on to the penetration of rifle 
bullets and the study of explosives. A short treatise on vibrations ex- 
pounded that dynamical subject on its practical side. By the numerous 
scientific memoirs that issued from the department its public reputation 
as a centre of scientific progress was maintained. 

Just before the outbreak of the war he was chosen as a member of the 
important Advisory Committee appointed to deal with a handsome endow- 
ment, provided by the Government for the promotion of science in its 
application to industry. But when war broke out nothing but active 
service would make him content, and he withdrew from all work not con- 
nected with military problems. He had prepared himself for taking his 
part in the National defence by organising and inspiring as Commanding 
Officer the Company of Engineers attached to the University Officers’ 
Training Corps. This work was not new to him. His father had been 
the leader in organising the body of Electrical Volunteers from the 
Engineering profession, and had been its first honorary Colonel. The 
son was active in following up this work, and furthered the erection of 
their headquarters: during the Boer War he assisted in the despatch of 
several contingents of this essential Corps to South Africa. He now took 
up a Commission in the Royal Engineers, and was assigned to experi- 
mental work and the supervision of the wireless telegraphy of the coast 
defences, afterwards passing on to work at the Admiralty. When the 
air forces began to assume an important place in the war, his special 
experience in the design and working of internal combustion motors 
marked him out for service with that arm. On the establishment of the 
Ministry, he was given a responsible position on the staff at Headquarters, 
finally with the rank of Lieutenant-Colonel, where he soon impressed the 


sur. 2. vou. 18. d 


XXXVI OBITUARY NOTICES. 


military and Parliamentary chiefs. It belongs to the business of an air 
officer, while discouraging bravado, not to fear risks or to restrict enter- 
prise ; his visits of inspection were usually made by air; on one of these 
numerous journeys while flying alone but with another machine travelling 
in company, an accident due to atmospheric causes suddenly terminated 
his brilliant career. 

The Air Council have taken the unusual and remarkable course of re- 
cording by public Resolution “ their deep sense of the high and permanent 
value of the work done for the Flying Forces by the late Colonel Hopkin- 
son, and their recognition of the private self-abnegation with which he 
devoted his great abilities and scientific attainments to the public service”: 
and they directed that there should be communicated to the Vice-Chancellor 
‘““an expression of profound regret at his untimely death and at the loss 
which has thereby fallen on the University of Cambridge.” Personal ex- 
pressions of regret and condolence have come from the highest in the 
land. 

He was elected F.R.S. in 1910. The C.M.G. came to him recently for 
public service. <A few years ago he became a Professorial Fellow of King’s 
College. On the social side his home, a centre of happiness and content, 
became also a centre of undergraduate interests, especially in recent years 
when his brother Cecil, who lived with his mother in the house adjacent, 
was at College. The death of this only remaining brother a short time 
ago, after a long illness ensuing from a severe wound received in Flanders, 
borne with cheerful resignation, completes this family history of sacrifice 
in the national service, which has left our country so much poorer. But 
it is only one chapter in the Cambridge record. 

Bertram Hopkinson had lived long enough to leave his mark perma- 
nently on his University. His life was lost in the military defence of his 
country, to which he had strenuously devoted all his powers at a crisis in 
the national fortunes. His death at the age of 44 years is, to his colleagues 
and to his friends, and not less to the University, an irreparable loss. 


J. L.

ON THE DYNAMICAL EQUATIONS OF THE TIDES 
Part I.—Tipau Systems witu Finite FREeEpom. 


By J. Provupman. 


[Read March 9th, 1916. — Received June 6th, 1916. — 
Received, in revised form, November 3rd, 1917. ] 


1. This is the preliminary part of an investigation in which the 
dynamical equations of the tides are obtained and used in Lagrange’s. 
form with an unlimited number of degrees of freedom. The transfor- 
mation to this form from the ordinary hydrodynamical one is made in 
Part II for flat seas, and in Part III for oceans on a sphere. 

The present part concerns the oscillations of a dynamical system of 
the same type as those provided by the tides, except that we take only 
a finite number of degrees of freedom. The reasoning is thereby not 
encumbered with questions of convergence, and this is the course which 
writers have usually pursued ; but some of the results cannot be applied 
to systems with infinite freedom without further justification. 

Investigations of Thomson and Tait, Lamb and Rayleigh, are brought 
together and extended so as to elucidate several aspects of the general 
theory, while tidal systems are reduced so that the coordinates can be 
separated into three discriminating classes. With the coordinates of the 
first class are associated ‘oscillations of the first class” ;* with those of 


* See, for instance, Lamb, Hydrodynamics, 3rd ed., p. 332; 4th ed., p. 338. 


SER. 2. vou.18. No. 1325. B 


2 Dr. J. ProupMan _ [March 9, 


the second class “ oscillations of the second class’ and free constant dis- 
placements ; with those of the third class free steady motions. 

By means of this classification tidal systems are themselves classified 
into systems of three types corresponding to a classification of oceans 
already given.* 

Further results are obtained concerning limiting forms of forced 
motion of long period, and slowly rotating systems.

When any coordinate is such that it occurs isolated in the expressions 
for both the kinetic and potential energies, z.c. when it only enters as do 
Pi» Po, ---» Pw in (18), we shall call it a ‘“ principal coordinate”. When 
the coordinates can be separated into classes such that modes of free 
oscillation exist in which only the coordinates of one class vary, we shall 
call such classes “fundamental classes”. When a fundamental class con-

When a coordinate is at the same time principal and fundamental, we 
shall call it a “normal coordinate”’.

Reduction of General System. 


4. We now return to the general system of $2 and suppose that 
principal coordinates are used. Let us separate these coordinates into 
classes, so that all the members of one class have equal coefficients of 
stability, and with different classes are associated different coefficients of 
stability. Consider the auxiliary system formed by suppressing all the 
coordinates except those belonging to one of the classes we have just 
defined. Such a system is of the special type of the preceding section, 
and its coordinates may be transformed into the fundamenta ones there 
shown to exist. The aggregate of the formule of transformation for all 
the different classes may now be used to transform the coordinates of the 
whole original system, and we shall suppose this done.

We shall now separate the coordinates /, of (38) into three classes. 
We shall say that f, is a “ coordinate of the first class” if the correspond- 
ing coefficient of stability is not zero ; we shall say that it is a “‘ coordinate 
of the second class” if the corresponding coefficient of stability is zero

efficient of stability and 6, are zero. 

The coordinates of the second class occur in associated pairs; in no 
case have we n’ =7. For any coordinate of the third class we may have 
n’ =n, in which case the coordinate will be a principal one.

When a system contains no coordinates of the third class, we shall say 
that it is of “ Type I’; when it contains coordinates of the third class but 
none of the second class, we shall. say that it is of “Type IL”; when it 
contains coordinates of both the second and third classes, we shall say that 
it.is of “ Type III”’. ; 

We shall always suppose that the components of disturbing force corre- 
sponding to coordinates of the second and third classes are zero, in order 
that our system may correspond to an actual tidal system.

Constant Displacements, Steady Motions and Limiting Forms. 


5. In the equations (42) let us suppose that F,, FP), ..., Fy, are con- 
-stants. 
If all the coordinates are of the first class, the solution of (42) will be 
° ie v3 ~ 
Je i, ES (52) 


vy, 


1916.] ON THE DYNAMICAL EQUATIONS OF THE TIDES. 15 


say, for >= 1, 2,...,.N,;. The system thus undergoes a constant dis- 
placement which vanishes with all the forces; this we shall call the 
“equilibrium solution.” 

If f_, is a coordinate of the second class, ie will be zero, but f_, may 
be any constant and its value will not affect the other coordinates. 

If f_, is a coordinate of the third class, f_, may be any constant, but 
its value will affect the coordinates of the first class. 

For a system of Type I, the coordinates of the second class may have 
any constant values, and (52) holds for those of the first class. The 
system thus undergoes a constant displacement and the coordinates of 
the first class have their equilibrium values. Free or forced steady 
motions are impossible. 

A system of Type II undergoes a steady motion in the coordinates of 
the third class, and a constant displacement in the coordinates of the first 
lass, but this constant displacement will not in general take the equi- 
librium form unless the steady motion vanishes. An independent free 
steady motion is possible corresponding to each coordinate of the third 
class. 

A system of Type IIL undergoes a steady motion in the coordinates of 
the third class, and a constant displacement in those of the other classes. 
‘The coordinates of the first class do not in general take their equilibrium 
values unless the steady motion vanishes. Free constant displacements 
and free steady motions are possible. 

Now let us consider the limiting form of forced periodic motion as the 
period tends to become infinite. The existence of unique limiting values 
of the coordinates of the first class, and of the speeds of the other coordi- 
nates, can be deduced from the general theory of forced oscillations of 
tidal systems with finite freedom.* 

Let us utilise the equations (45) and (50). We see that the limiting 
forms of qj, qo, ---» gx, Will be given by

Attention was first directed to steady motions and non-equilibrium 
limiting forms by Lamb*; he considered in particular a system of 
Type II with two degrees of freedom. Later Hough} considered systems 
of Types I and IT with two degrees of freedom, but took disturbing forces 
corresponding to his coordinates of the second class. Lord Rayleight 
afterwards considered a system of Type I with three degrees of freedom. 


Free Motion of Slowly Rotating System. 


6. Lord Rayleigh has discussed a tidal system with » small, and has 
found the effect of a small rotation on free modes which correspond re- 
spectively to only one and two coefficients of stability having a certain 
value. 

Now we may have more than two coefficients of stability equal, and 
in fact, in actual tidal systems an infinite number of them are zero. We 
shall now apply Rayleigh’s method to the general case. It will appear 
that no serious complication arises, however many coefficients of stability 
may be equal, so long as the values of (6, are distinct.

Now for a moment let us censider the general tidal system in which 
wis not small. The modes and speeds of oscillation are functions of 
and vary continuously with w. When » becomes small, their principal 
parts take the forms we have been investigating in the present section. 
We have seen that the principal part of the speed cannot contain a power 
of » higher than the first. When the principal part of the speed does not 
involve w, we shall eall the oscillation one of the “ first class’’ for all 
values of w; in this case the principal part of the motion for w small in- 
volves coordinates of the first class only. When the principal part of the 
speed contains w as a factor we shall say that the oscillation is of the 
“‘second class”’ for all values of w; in this case the principal part of 
the motion for w small involves coordinates of the second class only. We 
have already seen that with a coordinate of the second class we have 
associated a free constant displacement for all values of w, and that with 
a coordinate of the third class we have associated a free steady motion for 
all values of o. 

Oscillations of the second class ocenr in systems of Types I and III, 
but not in systems of Type II. 

The terms “oscillations of the first and second classes” were first 
used by Hough* in reference to tides in an ocean covering a rotating 


* ‘(On the Application of Harmonic Analysis. .., Part I1’’, Phil. Trans. R. 8., (A), 
‘Vol. 191, p. 159 (1897). 
c 2 


20 ON THE DYNAMICAL EQUATIONS OF THE TIDES. [March 9, 


globe. The existence of the two kinds had already been discovered by 
Lamb* for a particular flat circular sea of variable depth. In order to 
indicate the nature of the oscillations of the second class Lamb latert 
considered a system with two degrees of freedom like our tidal systems, 
except that », contained w as a factor. This, which is essential in Lamb’s 
case, means that », was derived entirely from centrifugal action. Now in 
the hydrodynamical cases the centrifugal terms had been neglected, so 
that it appears that oscillations of the second class can arise in a way 
other than that indicated by Lamb’s case of two degrees of freedom.. 
How this is so we have seen in the pee section.

ON THE DYNAMICAL EQUATIONS OF THE TIDES 


Parr 11.—Fuar Snas. 
By J. Proupman. 
[Read March 9th, 1916.] 


1. This part is concerned with flat rotating seas of any natural laws 
of shape and depth. The main result is a transformation of the differ- 
ential equations of motion into a set of the type considered in Part I, 
except that now we have an unlimited number of degrees of freedom. 

The algebraic linear equations which result from this transformation 
ean be explicitly constructed in a number of cases for which the tidal 
problem has not yet been solved. In a future part it is hoped to consider 
the application to some of these cases, but in the present part the solution 
is only discussed in cases where the depth is a function only of the dis- 
tance from a fixed point, and the boundaries are circles having this point 
as centre. This class includes all the cases of flat rotating seas for which 
the tidal problem has hitherto been completely solved; for each there 
exists an infinite set of fundamental classes of coordinates. 


General Differential Equations. 


2. We suppose that the bottom of the sea has everywhere a definite 
tangent plane, and that at the boundary line of the free surface the banks 
are not horizontal. We suppose also that the boundary line has a definite 
tangent at any point on it with the possible exception of a finite number 
of them. 

Let h denote the depth at any point below the plane of the free sur- 
face in equilibrium, and, using rectangular Cartesian axes in this plane, 
let 7, m denote the direction cosines of any outward drawn normal to the 
boundary. By o/on we shall denote differentiation along a normal to 
the boundary in the direction towards the boundary trom the sea, and by 
0/Os differentiation along the positive direction of the arc of the boundary. 

We shall often say that one function tends to another at the boundary, 


22 Dr. J. PRoupMAN } [March 9, 


and by this we shall mean that their difference tends to zero as the point 
at which they are evaluated tends to the boundary along every normal 
that exists. 

Unless otherwise stated, all sunaes integrals will be taken over the 
whole area of the surface of the sea, and all line integrals positively round 
the complete boundary, though, when the integrand of a line integral does 
not exist on the boundary, the limit as the boundary is approached along 
the normal is to be used.

From the way in which we have obtained the equations (87), it follows 
that they have a solution corresponding to every solution of the equations 
(23). We shall now proceed to show that every solution of the equations 
(87), which makes the series in (80) and (82) absolutely convergent, gives a 
solution of the equations of § 2. 

It follows from §§ 3, 4 that, when the series in (80) and (82) are abso- 
lutely convergent, functions

obtained will satisfy (74) and (75), while the functions ¢’ and W’ so ob- 


* See Note on previous page.

tained will satisfy (83) and (84). From the completeness of the systems 
of functions ¢, and y,, equations (87) for all positive integral values of 7 
require (23), and therefore we have a solution of the equations of § 2.* 
The equations (87) may therefore be taken as the equations of motion 
in the general tidal problem. We notice from (65)—(68), that they may 
be explicitly constructed when all the functions ¢, and yy, are known. 
We see from (79), (80), (85), and (86), that the equations (87) may be

there are no oscillations of the second class. The elimination of the co- 
ordinates of the third class in periodic motion can be effected exactly as 
in § 4 of Part I, and we obtain

’ We see at once from (65)—(68) that all the gyroscopic coefficients which

(66) and (67), so that we have no coordinates of the second class. The 
fundamental class forms a system of T’ype II, and the coordinates of the 
third class may be eliminated from the equations of motion.

ON THE DYNAMICAL EQUATIONS OF THE TIDES 


Parr IIJ.—Ocrans on a SPHERE. 


By J. Proupman. 
[Read March 9th, 1916.] 


1. In this part the results obtained in Part II for flat rotating seas are 
extended to oceans on a rotating sphere. 

Again, the algebraic linear equations which result from the transforma- 
tion can be constructed in a number of cases for which the tidal problem 
has not yet been solved. But in the present part their solution is only 
discussed in cases where the depth is a function of the latitude only, the 
boundaries, if any, being parallels of latitude. This class includes all the 
eases for which the tidal problem has hitherto been completely solved ; 
for each there exists an infinite set of fundamental classes of coordinates. 

In stating the analytical basis of the transformation, only such equa- 
tions as are afterwards quoted, or would be required in applications, are 
given. When the reasoning is exactly the same as in Part II, it is 
omitted. The numbering of sections and equations is throughout the 
same as that of corresponding sections and equations of Part I. 


General Differential Equations. 


2. Leét the radius of the sphere be denoted by a, and, using the axis 
of rotation as axis of spherical polar coordinates, let @ denote the co- 
latitude of any point on the sphere, and x the longitude of the point 
measured from some meridian fixed on the sphere. 

We suppose the ocean to satisfy the analytical conditions stated in 
§ 2 of Part II. 

Let h denote the depth at any point below the free surface in equili- 
brium, and 7, m the cosines of the angles which any outward drawn normal 
to the boundary makes with the directions respectively of 0, x increasing.

From the way in which we have obtained the equations (87) it follows 
that they have a solution corresponding to. every solution of the equations 
(23). We may also show, exactly as in Part II, that every solution of the 
equations (87) which makes the series in (80) and (82) absolutely conver- 
gent, gives a solution of the equation of § 2. 

The equations (87) may therefore be taken as the equations of motion 
in the general tidal problem. We notice from (65)—(68) that they may be 
explicitly constructed when all the functions of ¢, and yy, are known. 

We see from (79), (80), (85) and (86), that the equations (87) may be

7. For an ocean over which h sec @ is constant, we see from (68)’ that

there are no coordinates of the second class. The ocean forms a system 
of Type II, and there are no oscillations of the second class. The elimi- 
nation of the coordinates of the third class in periodic motion can be 
effected exactly as in §4 of Part I, and the equations (91) and (92) of 


_ Part I again apply. .

again apply.

Preliminary Reductions. 


1. In the Mathematische Annalen, Bd..56, Gordan has worked out a 
system of invariants for two quaternary quadratics. In the following 
pages it is proposed to shew how Gordan’s system can be very much 


1917. ] SIMULTANEOUS SYSTEM OF TWO QUADRATIC QUATERNARY FORMS. TL 


simplified. In fact, we shall reduce the system to 125 forms instead’ of 


Gordan’s 580 forms. 

The theorems established in Sections I-and II of this paper are sub- 
stantially the same as in Chapters 1 and 2 of Gordan’s work. They are 
only reproduced here to make the investigation complete.

Chains and Tags.* 
Further, let such a product as 


DM faa 2) Oe Oo 


be abbreviated to ( ; al ; ‘ : 


If this expression start with 7 and end with € or o, it is called a tag. 
We denote it by (¢). Otherwise it is a chain, when both the extreme 
symbols are of type € or o, or else neither €, w appear at all. We denote 
a chain by its extreme symbols 


ad (?)). (2%). ote 


Properties of Chains and Tags. 


16. (1) In one chain or tag all the upper symbols 7, k, m must differ, 
except possibly the two extremes of a chain. 


(2) So also must all the lower symbols 7, / differ. Otherwise the form 
is reducible. ‘Thus the chain 


(aur 
£218 


contains the factor ( 2 ay 
Peet 
(3) In a tag (2), each symbol 7, #, ... is paired, but 7 only occurs once. 


(4) In a chain (€, €) or (, ») each symbol is paired. 


(5) In a chain (€, o), (2, 7), or e Ab each symbol except the two ex- 
tremes is paired. 


(6) The symbols €, » can only occur as extreme symbols. 


* See note on previous page. 


80 : Mr. H. W. Turnpunn [Nov. 1, 


@ The Set of Forms J®, J, J®, 


17. It is obvious that all forms P which are made up of symbols’ 
tg, j@, kj, but which have no factor F', or Fy, are only of two kinds, viz. :— 


(I) chains such as (2 ae (2 da at Ge. a: 
(II) chains such as (‘ a” ar (F o 


For P consists of chains and tags only, and each extreme symbol of a 
chain or tag must be paired with some other extreme symbol elsewhere. 
The resultant of such can only be one chain. 


Reducibility. 


18. If by any process we can transform P to terms involving (1) either 
less symbols 1, 2, 8, or (2) more symbols €, w, then P is reducible. 


Proof.—(1) The number of pairs of symbols 1, 2, or 3 in P is the 
grade of P. To diminish the number of these symbols is to lower the 
grade. All we have to do is to deal with the forms in ascending value 
of grade. 


(2) If we consider the chains (I) before chains (II) of the same grade, 
then P is reduced if we increase the number of €, # symbols in P.

A MOVING ELECTRIC CHARGE AND ITS LINES OF ELECTRIC FORCE. 95 


THE NATURE OF A MOVING ELECTRIC CHARGE AND ITS 
LINES OF ELECTRIC FORCE 


By H. Bateman. 


(Read January 18th, 1916.—Enlarged May 18th, 1917, and May 9th, 1918.) 


1. Introduction. 


Recent developments in the theory of radiation point to the conclusion 
that the electromagnetic theory of radiation is at present incomplete, and, 
in his “ Report on Radiation and the Quantum Theory”, J. H. Jeans* 
suggests that Maxwell’s equations for the propagation of electric waves 
may need to be modified in such a way that the elementary electric charge 
¢ and Planck’s constant h will be involved explicitly. There is, of course, 
the alternative that ¢ and h may appear as constants of integration in an 
important type of solution of Maxwell’s equations, and the idea of a 
magneton has already been used with some success in electromagnetic 
theory.t It has been suggested by some that the atomic nucleus, by 
others that the electron is the ultimate magnetic particle. 

The idea of a magnetic electron} which consists of a thin ring of elec- 
tricity in rapid motion is gaining ground, and has already received some 
experimental support.§ The radius of the ring has indeed been esti- 
mated) to be about 2X10~-” cm., or about 500 times the radius generally 
adopted for a spherical electron. 

These new ideas concerning the form of the electron are very inter- 
esting, but they do not solve the fundamental problem relating to the 


* Proc. Phys. Soc., London (1914), p. 81. 

+ See, for instance, 8. B. McLaren, Brit. Assoc. Report, Birmingham (1913), p. 391: 
W. M. Hicks, Proc. Roy. Soc. (A), Vol. 90 (1914), p. 356; Vol. 91 (1915), p. 273. 

~ A. L. Parson, ‘‘A Magneton Theory of the Structure of the Atom’’, Smithsonian 
Publication, 2271, Washington (1915). 

§ A. H. Compton and O. Rognley, Phys. Review, Vol. 11 (1918), p. 122; A. H. Compton, 
J. Washington Academy of Science, Vol. 8 (1918), Jan, 4. 

|| A. H. Compton (l.c.). Parson estimated his magneton to have a radius of 1°5 x 10-%cm. 
D. L. Webster, Phys. Review, Vol. 9 (1917), p. 484, estimates the mass to be 2/c* times the 
electrostatic energy and gives a method of finding the thickness of the ring. 


96 Mr. H. Bareman (Jan. 18, 


manner in which an electron produces disturbances which are radiated 


outwards. To throw light on this question it seems necessary to frame _ 


some reasonable hypothesis as to the nature of the ether, and to make a 
careful study of Maxwell’s equations, for in spite of the objections which 
have been raised against them these equations still provide us with the 
best starting point, especially since they can be adapted to theories of 
both continuous and discontinuous media. 

The idea of a discontinuous ether may very well be the key to some 
of our difficulties in the theory of radiation, in fact in his report Jeans 
seems inclined to favour the idea of discrete Faraday tubes which has 
been so strongly recommended by Sir Joseph Thomson on many occasions,* 
and used as a basis for his theory of valency.t There is much to be said 
in support of such a theory. It is well known that the mathematical 
theory of electricity was much enriched when Maxwell translated Faraday’s 
ideas relating to lines of force into mathematical language, and when Sir 
Joseph Thomson continued the work by considering the case of moving 
lines of induction. In this way a vivid picture was obtained of the pro- 
cesses taking place in an electromagnetic field and light was represented 
geometrically as traverse oscillations travelling along the lines of force. 

These researches do not, however, exhaust the subject. We need to 
know more about the nature of the Faraday tubes, their connection with 
electric charges, their form in varying fields, and so forth. We need to 
know something about the origin of the potential energy in an electrostatic 
field, for mathematicians have thought for a long time that all energy is 
ultimately kinetic, and the importance of this way of looking at the matter 
has been emphasized by Sir Joseph Thomson.; Some of his remarks are 
of special interest in connection with the theory developed in this paper ; 
when considered in connection with his theory of moving Faraday tubes,§ 
they strongly suggest the conclusions that are indicated directly by certain 
solutions of Maxwell’s equations. 

In this paper we shall follow Sir Joseph Larmor|| in regarding Max- 
well’s equations for the ether as the starting point for the construction 
of more elaborate theories, and shall adopt.the idea that all electricity 


* Phil. Mag. (5), Vol. 31 (1891), p. 149; Vol. 19 (1910), p. 301; Vol. 26 (1913), p. 792: 
Proc. Camb. Phil. Soc,, Oct. (1907). 

+ The Corpuscular Theory of Matter, London (1907); Phil. Mag., Vol. 27 (1914), p. 757. 

+ Applications of Dynamics to Physics and Chemistry (1888), p. 15. 

§ As, for instance, by D. N. Mallik, Phil. Mag., Vol. 26 (1913), p. 144; Optical Theories, 
Camb. Univ. Press (1917). 

|| Alther and Matter, p. 77. 


1916.] A MovING ELECTRIC CHARGE AND ITS LINES OF ELECTRIC FORCE. 97 


arises from electrical separation.* ‘To elucidate this idea a special study 
will be made of certain solutions of Maxwell’s equations which seem to be 
of an elementary nature. The principal result of the investigation is 
that there is a type of radiant field which seems to be more fundamental 
than the electromagnetic field of a point charge of electricity, inasmuch as 
the flow of energy is rectilinear, and there is no transformation of energy 
outside the singularities of the field. The field of an electric pole moving 
with a velocity less than the velocity of light may, moreover, be obtained 
as the limiting case of two superposed fields of the type just mentioned, 
and the flow of energy in the field is generally of a complicated character ; 
so there seem to be some advantages in adopting the simpler type of field 
as fundamental. 

In order to obtain a clearer conception of the processes taking place 
in an electromagnetic field, we shall consider some types of fields in which 
there is a volume distribution of electricity, and shall introduce the idea 
of electric storage, a quantity whose rate of change is the volume density 
of electricity. Some geometrical theorems are obtained concerning the 
motion of lines of force, and these suggest very strongly that the lines of 
force are built up from particles of electricity. 


2. Solution of Maxwell's Equations. 


It the vectors E and H denote the electric and magnetic intensities in 
an electromagnetic field, and the units are chosen in a suitable way, Max- 
well’s equations for the ether may be written in the form

To solve the last equation we may use the method of Ampére* and

When a and @ are defined by these equations, the vector M given by

Maxwell’s equations is the most general solution which possesses this 
property ;* it can also be proved that if there are two solutions of Maxwell’s 
equations such that their vectors M and M’ are connected by a relation of 
type M’ = kM, where & is not a constant, then M? = 0 and the solution 
provided by either M or M’ is of the above type. 

A more general solution of Maxwell’s equations may be obtained by 
superposing a number of particular solutions of the present. type, and one 
of the objects of the present memoir is show how some important types 
of electromagnetic fields may be built up by superposition in this way. 
It should be mentioned that the field obtained by superposing an infinite 
number of singular radiant fields is not necessarily a field in which 
Maxwell’s equations are satisfied, for the singular curves of the radiant 
fields may fill the whole of space. It will be of some interest to aim at a 
second object and to endeavour to determine some of the properties of a 
general type of field that can be built up from singular radiant fields.

on the axis of z there is a radical component of E, but as the transverse 
components are now infinite there is no contradiction. The axis of z is a 
line of singularities and must be regarded as the locus of point charges of 
electricity which are moving away from the origin with the velocity of 
light; this at any rate is the correct interpretation when F(a, 8) has cer- 
tain forms. In some cases the moving singularities are magnetic poles 
instead of electric, while if F(a, 8) is a many valued function it may be 
difficult to give a physical meaning to the singularities. 

Let us now consider the simple case in which F(a, 8) depends only on 
a. There are then no other singularities in the field except perhaps those 
of the function F(a). Let us suppose moreover that F(a, 3) is zero except 
when a differs from ay by an infinitesimal amount: then M is infinite only 
at points on the axis of z which move according to the law |7| = ¢(¢—ap). 
If now we assume that these two points carry charges of electricity, it is 
easy to see that they must carry charges of opposite sign, for the surface 
integral of normal electric force over a sphere whose centre is at O is 
evidently zero if the radius of the sphere is so large that the moving 
charges have not reached it at the moment under consideration. It 
appears then that the singular radiant field arises from the breaking up 
at O of neutral particles into oppositely charged particles which fly away 
in opposite directions with the velocity of light. 

To verify this let us write F(a, 8) = /f'(a), where f’(a) is zero when

one direction by calculating the surface integral of the normal electric 
force over the zy plane. This surface integral is

The differentiation of the field may be regarded as the limit of an 
operation in which two singular radiant fields issuing from two neighbour- 
ing sources O and O’ on ths axis of z are superposed. The functions G(a) 
associated with the two sources are equal but of opposite signs, the magni- 
tude of G being F’/dz, where dz is the distance between O and O’. The 
above expressions indicate that, in the limit when dz is zero, the two super- 
posed radiant fields are equivalent to the electrostatic field of a point 
charge —F at O, but the equivalence is valid at time ¢ only for points 
which le within the sphere » = c(t—t). Outside this sphere there is 
no field at all. 

A similar result may be obtained by using Heaviside’s expressions for 
the electric and magnetic forces. H and H are both equal in magnitude 
to F/w, where w is the distance of a point (z, y, z) from the axis of z. 
The lines of magnetic force are circles in planes perpendicular to the axis 
of z, the centres of the circles being on the axis of z. The lines of electric 
force are circles in planes through the axis of z, their centres being at the 
point O. ‘The field is, of course, limited to a sphere of radius c (t—t)) 
with its centre at the origin. 

Now when two fields of this type are superposed so that their singular 
lines overlap, it is evident that, if the functions F are equal but of oppo- 
site signs, the magnetic forces in the two fields will cancel one another at 
ali points where both fields exist. The fragments of electricity fired out 
from O and O’ will also annul one another except at points of the interval 
OO’. The two superposed fields are consequently equivalent to the 
electrostatic field of a uniformly charged rod OO’, but this equivalence is 
valid only within the region common to two expanding spheres whose 
centres are at O and O'. 

If the separation of electricity begins at O and is initiated at O’ as 
soon as O’ is struck by a fragment of electricity from O, we shall have a 
constant electric charge associated with the interval OO’, and an equal 
and opposite charge spread over an interval of twice the length and 
travelling along the axis of z with the speed of light. This moving charge 
ought to be annulled in some way, and the simplest way of doing this is 
to introduce two other sources and Q’ at suitable points on the axis of 
z. In order that a cancelling may take place, the electric charge asso- 
ciated with Q and Q' must be equal and opposite to that associated with 


102 Me. H. Bateman (Jan. 18, 


O and O’, while the distance between Q and Q' must be the same as the 
distance between O and O’.

In these expressions 8, 8, 8,, and §, are functions of a, while r now

The singular radiant field specified by the above expressions has two 
singular curves, each of which is the locus of points travelling along 
straight lines with the velocity of light. These moving points may be 
supposed to be projected from the different positions of the primary 
singularity [€(a), »(a), ¢(a)] in directions specified by the unit vectors’ 8 (a) 
and 8, (a) respectively. 

The lines of electric force a = const., ¢ = const. form at any instant 
a system of circles meeting the two singular curves of the radiant field. 
The lines of magnetic force a = const., 6 = const. also form a system of 
circles. The lines of electric and magnetic force which lie on a sphere, 
with its centre at some position of the primary singularity, form two 
orthogonal systems of circles cut out by planes through two lines which 
are polar lines with respect to the sphere. 

From the expressions for E it is easy to prove that, if

the position at time ¢ of one of the singularities projected from the 
primary singularity at time a, and a force e/7, towards the position at 
time ¢ of the other singularity which was projected from the primary 
singularity at time a. 

The projected singular points evidently have electric charges associated 
with them, and as before we may imagine neutral particles to be con- 
tinually breaking up at the primary singularity into oppositely charged 
fragments which fly away with the velocity of light. To verify that this 
is the correct interpretation of our singular radiant field we may proceed 
as before, but the work is laborious. ‘The following method will, however, 
give us a partial verification.

Our previous statement regarding the electric intensity of the field in 
which concentrated charges are fired in two directions is now easily 
verified. 

The value of p and pv derived from the function Q are such that v is 
not generally equal to c the velocity of light. The reason is that when 
the original electricity which is created is moving outwards, a secondary 
creation of electricity takes place in the region traversed, and some of 
this new electricity converges towards a new position of the primary singu- 
larity and tends to produce a steady state in which electricity of a con- 
centrated type is continually formed. This phenomenon seems to be 
connected in some way with the permanence of electric charges, and is 
illustrated by a geometrical theorem concerning lines of force which will 
be proved presently. | 


5. The Interference of Singular Radiant Fields and the Cancelling 
of Singularities.

overlap. Let O and O, be the primary singularities: then these must

O,, and electricity fired from O, in one direction hits O. The other diree- 
tions in which electricity is fired from O and O, must coincide with the 
directions of motion of the particles of electricity which hit O and QO, 
respectively.

Directions of projection which are associated in this way have already

6. A Geometrical Theorem relating to Lines of Electric Force. 


Let us regard a, y, z and ict as rectangular coordinates in a space of 
four dimensions. ‘Then the path of a moving electric pole is represented 
by a curve in the space S,, and the path of a light-particle is represented 
by an isotropic line in this space. 

Let P be a point on this curve, PQ an isotropic line through P, and 
P', P" two points on the curve which are consecutive to P. We may draw 
a sphere through P@P’P”, and this sphere will contain the isotropic 
line PQ. 

Let P’Q’ be an isotropic line consecutive to PQ which also lies on the 
sphere: then if P, P’, P’’, P’’’, are consecutive points on our curve we may 
draw a sphere through P’Q’P"P’” and determine an isotropic line through 
P" which hes on this new sphere, and so on. 

Proceeding in this way we may assign an isotropic line for each point 
of the curve, and this means that we have a rule for assigning the direc- 
tion of projection of a light-particle for each position of the electric pole. 

Now a sphere in Sy, corresponds to a hyperboloid of revolution in 
ordinary space and an isotropic line on the sphere to a generator of the

March 1915.

three consecutive positions of the moving electric pole.

The theorem that has just been proved may also be interpreted geo- 
metrically as follows :— 


_ Let P,P, Ps be three consecutive positions of the moving electric pole, 
and let P,Q, PaQs be consecutive directions of projection associated with 
the same lune of electric force. Then it is possible for light-particles 
which start from P,, Py respectively at times 7,, T:, to reach Q; and Qs at 
times ty, te respectively, while a light-particle which starts from Q, at 
time t, arrives at the points Qs, and Ps at times ty, Ts respectively. 


1916.] A MOVING ELECTRIC CHARGE AND ITS LINES OF ELECTRIC FoRcE. 115 


The light-particles are supposed to travel along straight lines with the 
velocity of light, and the theorem tells us that the light-particle which 
arrives at P, encounters on its way the light-particles which start from 
P, and P,. 

The fact that P,Q,, P.Q, are generators of a hyperboloid of revolution 
which passes through P, gives an interesting meaning to associated direc- 
tions of projection, for two associated directions of projection through P, 
are generators of the same hyperboloid of revolution. We also see that 
the light-particles which start from the points P,, Py, Ps, give one line 
of force, while the light-particles which come to P,, P:, Ps will, if they 
continue their paths unbroken, give an associated line of force. 

The circumstance that light-particles appear to flow both to and from 
the moving electric pole is exactly what we should expect when we con- 
sider the field of the electric pole to be built up in the manner described 
in §§ 4 and 5, for it may be recalled that, in the field wherein concentrated 
charges are created and shot out in only one direction which varies with 
the time, electricity must be supposed to flow towards the primary singu- 
larity as soon as the creation of electricity has been initiated. This elec- 
tricity is indeed generated by a secondary creation or breaking up of 
doublets that is initiated by the free electricity that has already been 
formed, and this inward flow of electricity tends to secure the permanency 
of the electric charge associated with the electric pole. 

It should be remarked that according to the present theorem a line of 
electric force in the field of an electric pole always consists of the same 
particles of electricity, but these particles are arranged so that positive 
and negative charges cancel, and so there is no volume distribution of 
electricity. The electric charges travel along straight lines with the 
velocity of light, and the law of variation of these lines is given by our 
geometrical theorem. 

An alternative proof of this theorem is given in the paper referred 
to on p. 99.

This field has singularities only on a curve of finite length joining the 
twe moving points O and O,. It should be noticed that it is much easier 
to bring about a cancelling of singularities in the present case because 
each primary singular point has only one singular curve attached to it, so 
that the function p, can vary in an arbitrary manner, and is not restricted 
like the function f(r) which occurs in § 5. 

In a field of the present type the light-particles which are fired out 
from O may be regarded as either electric or magnetic doublets or a com- 
bination of both. This explains why it is not necessary for particles to be 
fired out in two directions as in the case when the particles are charged ; 
it is easy, however, by a suitable combination of fields of this type to 


1 
| 1916.] A MovING ELECTRIC CHARGE AND ITS LINES OF ELECTRIC FoRcE. 117 


| obtain a field in which doublets are fired out in any number of directions 
from a moving point O. 
| It is quite possible, though not likely, that ultra-violet light and X-rays 
consist of a combination of two types of radiation, namely the ordinary 
radiation from an electron whose motion is accelerated, and a singular 
radiant field of the present type. The presence of the moving doublets 
would be in accordance with some ideas of Prof. W. H. Bragg* that have 
now been almost abandoned. The dual nature of X-rays was suggested 
by O. Heaviside+ soon after Bragg had proposed the doublet theory, but 
Heaviside considered the case in which the corpuscles carried along by the 
waves are charged particles.{ The singular radiant field would in this case 
be of the type considered in §§ 4 and 5. 

If the dual nature of light and X-rays is ever considered to be estab- 
lished, the present analysis may be of some interest as it provides a 
possible means of accounting mathematically for the absorption or deflec- 
tion of the corpuscular radiation by means of the phenomenon of the 
cancelling of singularities. 


8. Some Speculations on the Structure of Aither and Matter. 


To avoid circumlocution, a field in which there is a continual separa- 
tion of electricity at a point O, which is either at rest or in motion, will be 
called w field of Heaviside’s type. As in § 4, the two oppositely charged 
particles of electricity which are produced at any instant are supposed to 
move away from the source O with the velocity of light, describing recti- 
linear paths. 

We have seen that a field of Heaviside’s type can be regarded as the 
result of superposing two fields in which the volume distributions of elec- 
tricity cancel. The component fields will be called logarithmic fields, and 
will be regarded here as more fundamental than the field of an electric 
pole, because they give some indication of the electrical nature of the 
ether and the manner in which electric charges are maintained ; moreover, 
as we have already seen, the field of a moving electric pole may be re- 
garded as a limiting case of four superposed fields of this type. 

We shall now adopt the hypothesis that matter is composed of the 
primary singularities of logarithmic fields, and that the ether is produced 
by the electricity which is fired out from these sources after it has been 


* Proc. Royal Institution, Jan. 1911. 

+ Electromagnetic Theory, Vol. 3, p. 477. 

t Some cases of fields of this type have also been considered by Levi Civita, Comptes 
rendus, t, 145 (1907), pp. 417-420; Rend, Lincet, Vol. 18 (1909), p. 83. 


118 Mr. H. Bateman [Jan. 18; 


created there. The ether must also be supposed to contain the electricity 
which is created by the electricity travelling outwards from the sources. 
This moving electricity seems indeed either to induce a separation of 
electric charges in the sense of a true creation, or to break up doublets 
which are already present in the ether on account of the existence of other 
sources. We are unable to decide at present which view is correct, as the 
true nature of electricity is not understood. If an electric charge is simply 
a place where some quantity is a maximum or minimum, then new electric 
charges can come into existence in pairs just as maxima and minima can 
be created together, and in this connection it is interesting to note that 
new maxima and minima often come into existence where a maximum or 
minimum already exists. 


This analogy is, however, rather far fetched, Sg sis

be avoided at points not occupied by matter if there is a complete can- 
celling of singularities, but we shall find it useful fo consider the general 
case when the cancelling is not complete.

If we regard z, y, z and ct as the rectangular coordinates of a point 
in a space of four dimensions, these equations imply that the surfaces 


V = const., t= const. are orthogonal to the surfaces X = const., ~ 


Y = const. 

It should be noticed that the equations X = const., Y = const. repre- 
sent a moving line of electric force, while the equations V = const., 
7 = const. represent a moving line of magnetic force. Hence in a field 
of Thomson’s type, the moving lines of electric and magnetic force have, 
as their images in a space of four dimensions, two systems of surfaces 
such that any member of one family is orthogonal to any member of the 
other. 

One way of obtaining two families of surfaces of this type is to take a 
quadruply orthogonal system of hyper-surfaces (varieties) in S,. If the 
equations of the hyper-surfaces are X = const., Y= iconst.;_V = consth 
‘7 = const., respectively, then the surfaces X = const., Y = const. are 
orthogonal to the surfaces V = const., tT = const. 

It is known that any singly infinite system of hyperspheres belongs to 


a quadruply orthogonal system,* and that the hyperspheres are mapped 


conformally on oue another by their orthogonal trajectories. The remain-

ing members of the quadruply orthogonal system can be determined by 
finding a triply orthogonal system of surfaces in one hypersphere ; the 
hyper-surface formed by drawing orthogonal trajectories through points 
of one of these surfaces then belongs to the quadruply orthogonal system. 

Now it is known that any singly infinite system of spheres on a hyper- 
sphere forms part of a triply orthogonal system, and the orthogonal trajec- 
tories through the points of one of these spheres will cut all the other 
hyperspheres in spheres. Hence it is possible to find two orthogonal 
systems of ” surfaces in which one system consists entirely of spheres.

general theorem that has been proved for fields of Thomson’s type.*

closed curve without losing energy by radiation. This result may be of 
some interest in connection with Bohr’s theory of atomic structure. 


11. L[nfomtesimal Transformations associated with Lines of Force. 


In analytical dynamics the idea of representing the motion of a system 
of particles by a succession of infinitesimal transformations is a familiar 
one. A similar idea can be used with advantage in the theory of moving 
Faraday tubes. We must endeavour to find an infinitesimal transforma- 
tion which will send the singularity (€, 7, ¢, 7) into a consecutive position, 
and each direction of projection of a light-particle into a corresponding 
direction of projection of a light-particle, the two directions being asso- 
ciated with the same line of electric force. We shall, moreover, choose 
the transformation so that a light-particle travelling along a straight line 
with the velocity of light corresponds to another light-particle travelling 
along a straight line with the velocity of light. The infinitesimal trans- 
formation and the motion of the light-particles may then be regarded as 
two permutable motions, both of which are possible motions for particles 
which always belong to the same Faraday tube. It is known that a 
spherical wave transformation establishes a correspondence between points 
which describe straight lines with the velocity of light. Now if r denotes 
the radius vector from the origin of coordinates to a point with coordinates

12. Coordinated Motions of Point Charges. 


Let us consider the properties of a system of electromagnetic fields of 
the type considered in § 10 when the singular points move along the path 
curves of a succession of infinitesimal normal transformations. 

Let EH and ZH’ be the positions of two of the singular points for a given 
value of e«. Then if particles, fired from these positions at the appropriate 
times, collide after travelling along straight lines with the velocity of fight, 
this property will be invariant under the infinitesimal transformation 
which sends # and H’ into their consecutive positions. Since, moreover, 
the consecutive direction of projection derived by the infinitesimal trans- 
formation corresponds to the same line of electric force as the original 
direction of projection, the preceding result indicates that there is perma- 
nent incidence between certain lines of electric force issuing from HH 
and EH’. 

When £ and Z’ are given, the possible points of collision lie on a 
quadric of revolution with H and EH’ as foci; hence there is permanent 
incidence between each line of force from H and at least one line of force 

spR. 2. vou. 18. No. 1333. K 


130 Mr. H. Bateman [Jan. 18, 


from E'. When this relation holds, the two electromagnetic fields with # 
and H' as singularities will be said to be partially mcident. For com- 
plete incidence there must be permanent incidence between each line of © 
force from ZH and an infinity of lines of foree from H’. This case arises 
when the infinitesimal transformation is the same for all values of e, because 
then we can apply the above reasoning to any two positions of H and EH’. 

Let us now consider the hypothesis that the different point charges 
composing an electron move in such a way that there is partial incidence 
between the fields of each pair of point charges. This condition may be 
satisfied by making the point charges move along the path curves of a 
succession of normal infinitesimal transformations, provided the vector a 
determined by the transformations is proportional to the acceleration of 
the point charge at the point under consideration. An appropriate

13. Some Thoughts on Gravitation and the Apparent Constancy of the 
Electrome Charge. 


Let us consider two fields of Heaviside’s type whose singular curves 
overlap without producing a complete cancelling of singularities. If the 
two primary singularities O and O, are very close together, we shall have 
a variable electric charge associated with the short interval OO,, and at 
any instant this is equal but opposite in sign to the total charge asso- 
ciated at that instant with the two singular curves. It is possible also to 
imagine a case in which each singular curve carries the same charge, and 
indeed such an arrangement seems a very natural one on account of 
symmetry. 


* American Journal, Vol. 34 (1912), p. 325. 


132 Mr. H. Bateman (Jan. 18, 


We may imagine then a type of electron whose charge fluctuates round 
a mean value.* Two singular curves must be supposed to be attached to 
the electron, and we shall suppose that when the charge on the electron is 
2f(t) the charge on each singular curve is —/(d). ; 

If Q is any point on a singular curve and 7 is the instant at which a 
fragment of electricity must leave O in order to arrive at @ at time ¢, the 
charge associated at time t with the portion from Q to infinity of the 
singular curve is —/f(r). Let us now consider a second electron or posi- 
tive nucleus of an atom at @, and suppose that one of its singular curves 
coincides with the portion from Q to infinity of O’s singular curve. Then 
there will be a complete cancelling of singularities on this portion of the 
singular curve if Q’s singular curve carries a charge f(r) at time ¢. If Q, 
like O, has two singular curves attached to it and the charges carried by 
them at any instant are equal, then the charge associated with @ at time 
t must be —2f (7), and, like the charge associated with O, it must also 
fluctuate round a mean value. If now the extent of the fluctuation is 
negligibly small, the mean value of the charges at O and Q will be 
approximately equal, but of opposite signs. 

The second singular curve from @ may be supposed to go to some 
other electron P, whose charge at time 4 will be likewise 2f(7), ¢, being 
the time associated with ¢. Similarly O’s second singular curve may be 
supposed to be a continuation of one from another point N, whose charge 
at time ¢) is —2f(r), and so on. 

It is not necessary, of course, to limit the number of singular curves 
issuing from an electron to two, for we may imagine the field of an electron 
or positive nucleus to be built up from any number of fields of Heaviside’s 
type in which the primary singularities are very close together. We may, 
in fact, imagine a type of electron with singular curves extending in all 
directions, and may assume that the electric charge associated with each 
singular curve at any instant is a constant fraction of the charge asso- 
ciated with the electron at that instant. If now all the electrons and 
positive nuclei in the universe are connected together by singular curves 
so that there is a cancelling of singularities which will prevent electricity 
from going to infinity, it is easy to see from the foregoing analysis that 
there may be a mathematical reason why all the electrons in the universe 
carry the same mean charge. 

It should be mentioned that it is not necessary to suppose that all the 
particles.in the universe are connected together by singular curves made 
up of uncompensated electric charges, for we may imagine that when a 


* The question whether an electron can lose some of its charge was raised by Heaviside (/.c.).
