neutral “ ether particle” is produced by a “cancelling of singularities” 
or a union of oppositely charged fragments of electricity, this sther 
particle breaks up again into the same oppositely charged fragments at 
a source of a singular radiant field. Let us then consider three sources 
O, O,, Oz of singular radiant fields, and suppose that the first two fields 
interfere with one another and furnish “ether particles”? which break 
up at the third source. 

Let a fragment of electricity start from O at time 7, let it arrive at 
O; at time 7, and combine with an oppositely charged fragment of elec- 
tricity : finally let it part from its companion at O, at time 7, It is 
clear then that if the ether particles which break up at O, have been 
furnished by O and QO, since the beginning of time, the charge associated 
with one of O,’s singular curves is equal at time 7, to the charge asso- 
ciated with one of O’s singular curves at time rT. 

Now the charge associated with O’s singular curves at any instant 
may be supposed to determine in some way the charge at this instant on 
the electron or positive nucleus of which O forms part. Similar remarks 
apply to O, and O,, and so there may be a definite relation between the 
magnitudes of the charged particles containing O, O, and O, respectively, 
whether these particles carry charges of the same or opposite signs. 

This idea of the fluctuation of the electric charge associated with a 
particle of matter leaves a road open for a possible explanation of gravita- 
tion. Let us consider two particles A and B at associated times 7 and f, 
that is at times such that a light-particle starting from A at time 7 would 
arrive at B at time ¢. We shall suppose that the charge on A at time +r 
differs from its mean or normal value e¢ by xv(7), and that the charge on 
B at time ¢ differs from its normal value e’ by —x,y(r) where « and x, 
are constants. This means that the variation in B’s charge is proportional 
to the variation which would be produced if a percentage of the charge 
fired out from A went direct to B. 

If we now suppose that y(t) varies periodically with a period 27’, the 
mean value of the electric force exerted by A on B will be, according to 
- the inverse square law,

The idea that gravitation may be due to a periodic disturbance is not 
new. Hicks,* Whittaker,+ Korn,{ and others, have suggested theories of 
this type. The present suggestion is also analogous to the well-known 
theory in which the attraction between two charges of opposite sign is 
supposed to be greater than the repulsion between two similar charges of 
the same magnitudes as the others. This theory, which is generally attri- 
buted to Mosotti, is mentioned in Maxwell’s Electricity and Magnetism. 
It has been discussed recently by Lorentz,§ Gans, J. J. Thomson,4 and 
D. L. Webster.** |

2. This principle may be used for the purpose of constructing a 
mechanism which will trace out a three-bar curve. If D, HE, F are three 
points, one on each of the sides of the focal triangle OO'O", and we con-

they are the vertices of a pedal triangle at all, they will be those of the 
pedal triangle of a point on a three-bar curve. The only condition that 
they must observe in order that they should form a pedal triangle is that 
the perpendiculars through them to the sides of the focal triangle should 
meet in a point. This can be ensured in the following manner (see 
figure). 


Let the rod FG be made to slide along the side OO’. Attach to it a 
Peaucellier cell PQG whose fixed pivots are at Q and G, and which is 
arranged so that the point P describes a straight line through F, perpend-

have any form. The moving links of the three-bar linkage will be respec-

been thought necessary to show these cells in the figure.

ON THE CONNEXION BETWEEN LEGENDRE SERIES AND 
FOURIER SERIES 


By Pror. W. H. Youna. 


[Received November 1st, 1917.—Read December 6th, 1917.] 


1. The results hitherto obtained in the theory of the convergence of a 
Legendre series, defined as a series of the form 


2b a), (1) 


1 
where = +H | RCO MER COE HDs (2) 
P,,(z) being Legendre’s 7-th coefficient, namely the coefficient of a” in the 
expansion of (l—2axz-+a”)~? in powers of z, are somewhat isolated; and a 
theory of the convergence of a series of these polynomials of the form (1), 
which does not satisfy (2), series which are not Legendre series,* has rot, 
as far as I am aware, been developed at all. 

In the present memoir I propose to illustrate a method leading to re- 
sults which not only include as more or less special corollaries all the 
theorems already known on the subject of the convergence of these and 
kindred series, but, at the same time, places their theory on a footing as 
firm as that of trigonometrical series, at least in so far as the wmternal 
points of the interval considered are concerned. The end-points of the 
interval (—1 and 1 in the case of Legendre series) being, in general, 
singular, require, of course, a special treatment varying with the particular 
type of series, and in the present communication I have accordingly not 
introduced them, reserving their discussion for a subsequent paper. 

Our method owes its power to the introduction of what I have called 
Restricted Fourier Series, and to the fundamental theorems which I have 
been able to establish with regard to them, theorems, the most striking of 
which I have quite recently communicated to the Society. We are thus 
enabled to pass from trigonometric series of the restricted Fourier form 


* We distinguish between a Legendre series and a series of Legendre coefficients. 


142 Pror. W. H. Youne [ Dec. 6, 


to:Legendre series and other series of an extremely general type, with 
almost the same ease as it is known we can pass to Sturm-Liouville 
series from ordinary Fourier series. This facility must not be judged by — 
the length at which the present paper has been written out. The reason- 
ing is indeed intuitive in character and the conclusions become almost 
self-evident when it has been properly grasped. 

The great advantage of Restricted Fourier Series, and more especially 
of what I called ordinary Restricted Fourier Series, or R. F. series, is that, 
in the interval, or intervals, to which we restrict them, they behave more 
or less like Fourier series. The convergence properties of R. F. series in 
particular are practically the same as those of Fourier series. 

Our method then has the advantage of allowing the student who is 
acquainted with the theory of Fourier series to see the almost intuitive 
character of the theorems, and to memorise them without separate enun- 
ciation. The use of elaborate analysis, such as has been so ingeniously 
employed hitherto, is rendered unnecessary, equally whether the conver- 
gence required is ordinary, or uniform, or belongs to one of the scales of 
Cesaro summations, with index integral or fractional. And the series thus 
rendered manageable are more general in type than those yet considered. 
They include, for example, not only all convergent Legendre series, but 
every convergent series of Legendre coefficients whose integrated series 
converges to an integral in some interval internal to (-1<a2< 1) and 
containing the point or points under consideration. But further, the nature 
of the reasoning is such that the present theory may be regarded as 
fairly typical of the general theory of higher series, whether of normal 
functions or of more general functions with trigonometric asymptotic 
approximations. The whole fabric of the theory of these series, with their 
important applications to mathematical physics, is thus made to repose on 
the Theory of Fourier Series. 

The main theorem obtained in the present paper is as follows :— 


At any internal point, or in any internal closed interval, of the in- 
terval (-1<2<1), the series of Legendre (1) behaves in respect of 
convergence, divergence, or oscillation, uniform or otherwise, precisely like 
the Fourier serves of f (cos 9), where 

z= cos, 
provided the ndiwidual terms of the serves (1) tend to zero, that is to say, 
provided 


nQ,270 (n>). (3) 


The theorem still holds for a series of Legendre’s coefficients of the form 


1917.] Connexion BerwEEN LEGENDRE SERIES AND Fourter surizs. 148 


(1), provided the first integrated series converge to an integral in some 
interval containing the point, or points, under consideration, or, what is 
the same thing, if the sum of the second integrated series [necessarily 
convergent im virtue of (8)] ts in such an interval the integral of an 
integral.* 

This latter class of series, as is shewn in § 18, includes; as a special 
case, Legendre series for which (8) holds. 

It follows in particular from this theorem that the upper and lower 
functions of a Legendre series (1), satisfying (8), as well as (2), at an in- 
ternal point of the interval (—1, 1), depend only on the values of f(x) in 
a neighbourhood of the point considered as small as we-please, and are 
given as the upper and lower limits, as > ©, of the expression 


dt 
x—t’ 


B+ e 
3 (n+ 1) { J {Pur (at) Pr (t)—Pa(a) Pai} fO 
é being any fixed quantity, as small as we please. 

In an Appendix, I give an independent proof of this theorem based on 
different and more elementary considerations. 

In basing our theory on the main theorem of the present paper, we 
have, as it will be observed, rendered it possible to incorporate, without 
further analysis, in the theory of our series, any convergence criterion for 
Fourier series which may in the future be discovered. Applying those 
tests which are at present known, we have, moreover, at once a number 
of results which need not be enumerated here. I may, however, perhaps 
remind the reader that all the known tests are summed up in the two 
given respectively by de la Vallée Poussin and myself :— 

(1) x \. {fia@twtf(c—wu)} duis a function of bounded variation ; 
the Fourier serves of f(z) then converges to

(2) 4{f@twtfe-—w} >, (09),

* The special case of the theorem dealing with the Legendre series of a function whose 
square is swummable had been proved by quite different methods, adapted ad hoc, by A. Haar 
(Math. Annalen, Vol, 78, 1918, pp. 121-136). 


144 Pror. W. H. Youne [Dec. 6, 


It follows then from the results of the present paper that, under the 
same circumstances, the Legendre series of f(x) converges, at any point « 
internal to (—1 < 4 <1), to the same value as the Fourier series, and 
that the difference of corresponding partial summations tends uniformly 
to zero. We thus get tests for the convergence for a Legendre series, 
neither of which has as yet been established for any but Fourier series 
and R. F. series. 

I have not thought it wise to lengthen the paper by dealing explicitly 
with Cesaro convergence. No fresh difficulty is introduced, if we do so, 
except that the corresponding theorems regarding the Cesaro convergence 
of Restricted Fourier Series would be required, and that a closer asymp- 
totic formula, the theory of which may now be regarded as fairly com- 
plete, would need to be applied for the higher indices. 

It has already been remarked that the method here used is fruitful in 
connexion with series of many other types. ‘There are special reasons, 
however, for considering Legendre series first and separately. After 
Fourier series, they are perhaps the best known of all series of real func- 
tions, and are calculated to interest a large class of readers. Moreover, in 
their treatment the use of the complex variable is entirely dispensed with, 
whereas, in applying the method to other expansions, more or less well 
known, it is often convenient to employ the method of residues for the 
expansion of a suitable power of the independent variable, so as to be 
able, as in § 38, to apply Lerch’s theorem. I propose to discuss elsewhere 
some of the series here contemplated.

THEoREM.—If a series of Legendre polynomials (not necessarily a 
Legendre series) See) (1)

converges to an integral in some sub-interval of (—1, 1), not including

In fact (14) holds as an actual equation in our interval, and the right- 
hand side is an integral, since the proof of this relation (14), as well as the 
proof that ¢, and ¢, are integrals, do not involve the fulfilment of the 
relation (8). 

But a trigonometrical series which converges to an integral is an R. F. 
series. * 

Thus the series on the left of (14) is an R. F. series, and therefore— 
since it converges to an integral, and behaves like a Fourier series—con- 
verges uniformly throughout the sub-interval in question. Hence also 
the series on the right converges uniformly. This proves the theorem.

1. The theory of these series as hitherto exposed may be regarded as 
involving serious difficulties of analysis. ‘Till quite recently Dini’s work 
constituted the last word on the subject. Previously a long series of 
mathematicians had occupied themselves with the question, including 
names like Fourier, Hankel, and Harnack. Yet Dini seems to have been 


1917. ] ON SERIES OF BussEL FUNCTIONS. 165 


the first to obtain reliable results. The conditions due to him are some- 
what complicated in character, and, in the light of recent advances in 
Analysis, more especially in the theory of Fourier series, far from final. 
Kneser has availed himself of the modern subject of Integral Equations 
to obtain results of an isolated nature for Bessel series }A,Jn(k,x), where 


1 
A= Cp \ Shr L) LY ae) an, 
for the case m = 0, and Hobson, basing himself, as he tells us, partly 
on Kneser’s work, has proved that the Bessel series of a function f(x) con- 
verges at every point of the half-closed interval (0< x <1) at which the 
function has bounded variation, provided only that ./x f(z) is summable. 

I have already, in a-paper dealing with series of Legendre’s Poly- 
nomials, explained a general method of treating the expansion of f(z) in 
a series of functions, based on a fundamental theorem which I have 
recently discovered in the theory of what I have called Restricted Fourier 
Series. The application to Legendre’s polynomials exhibits the method 
in a peculiarly pure light, as the asymptotic approximation for P,, (x) 
involves trigonometrical functions of the type employed in harmonic 
trigonometrical series, viz. sinnx and cosnz, where n is an integer. 
Moreover the fact that the functions in terms of which we expand are 
polynomials enables us to avoid entirely the use of the complex variable. 

In order then to exhibit the full power of the method, it has seemed 
to me desirable to discuss a case where these simplifications do not pre- 
sent themselves. I have chosen the theory of series of Bessel functions 
DA,Jm(k, x) with this object in view, and the more readily because, as 
has been just pointed out, so little has hitherto been achieved in the 
theory of these series. 

I have been at pains to explain the processes employed as carefully as 
possible, so as to render them intelligible to those who are unacquainted 
with previous work on the subject. There is indeed little connexion be- 
tween the work here exposed and that of previous authors. 

As regards the conclusions arrived at, and indicated, these may, I 
think, fairly be said to be of a far-reaching nature, and constitute little 
less than the complete solution of the problem of determining the be- 
haviour of the. series under consideration in the matter of convergence, 
uniform convergence, convergence by the methods of Cesaro, oscillation, 
and so forth, at any internal point of the interval 0,1). Thus it is shown 
that, under the sole condition—necessary for convergence—that the typical 
term of a Bessel series tends to zero, the series behaves at any such internal 


166 Pror. W. H. Youne [Dee. 6, 


point precisely like a Fourier series, and we may obtain, without difticulty, 
circumstances under which it will converge in the Cesadro manner, from a 
knowledge of the power to which we must raise the inverse of the index 
expressing position in the series in order that the typical term, multiplied 
by this power, should tend to zero. On the other hand very general 
sufficient conditions, which the function F(a) must satisfy in order that the 
coefficients may be of the required order, can be at once written down. 
One of the more interesting features of the discussion is perhaps the 
introduction of trigonometrical series of the non-harmonic type 


XY {ay cos (K-+n) e+, sin(k+nz t ; 


which are shown to have properties analogous to those possessed by 
ordinary trigonometrical series, equally whether & be rational or irra- 
tional. 

Again, it is of importance to remark that the scope of the investigation 
does not only include series of the type hitherto studied (Bessel series) in 
which the coefficients are expressible by means of integrals involving the 
function f(z). Our method gives results of scarcely less importance when 
the coefficients are not of this form (series of Bessel functions), In accord- 
ance with the analogy suggested by the step taken in passing from ordinary 
Fourier series to Restricted Fourier Series. In particular we are able to 
deal with the derived series of Bessel series, although such derived series 
in our case necessarily cease to have the same form as the series from 
which they are derived. We are thus able to complete in a notable point 
the usual investigations in the applications. In fact, ¢¢ has beer usual to 
assume tacitly that, if the individual term satisfies a certain differential 
equation, the function represented by the sum of an infinite sertes of such 
terms will also satisfy it. It ts here shewn that such a coneluston ts 
legitimate, if the function f(x) have, in the interval mr which such an 
equation ts to be satisfied, a differential coefficient of suitable order, such 
that the Fourier series of a function equal to this differential coeficient 
in this interval, and having any convenient values elsewhere, converges tr 
the interval in question. This ts, for instance, the case if the differential 
coefficient has bounded varration. 

It* will be seen that our method, based as it is on the asymptotic 
trigonometric approximation [(viii) of § 4], which only holds in an inter- 
val excluding the origin, gives us no information as to what happens at 
z=0. When m> 0, the series obviously converges there to zero, but 


. 


* Added January 29th, 1918. 


1917.] On seRIES oF BESSsEL FUNCTIONS. 167 


it remains an open question as to the conditions under which the conver- 
gence is bounded in the neighbourhood, or, in the case when the function 
approaches zero as limit there, uniform. On the other hand we are able, 
by considerations analogous to those employed below, to deal with the end- 
point z = 1, obtaining a result exactly corresponding to that for an in- 
ternal point of the interval (0, 1). The exposition of these latter con- 
siderations involves, however, a more detailed treatment of the theory of 
non-harmonic trigonometrical series than has been found possible to 
include in the present communication.* The question, moreover, of con- 
vergence of a series, at the extremity of an interval in which it defines an 
associated function, requires and deserves a fuller and separate discussion, 
and I propose shortly to make a communication to the Society on these 
and kindred matters. 

On the other hand, in view of the fact that I have not found it con- 
venient to dispense entirely with the use of the complex variable, I have 
thought it well to shew in an Appendix what additional considerations, 
drawn from the theory of the complex variable alone, and avoiding, in 
particular, the use of the theory of Integral Equations, suffice for the 
proof of the known theorem that a Bessel series converges at z= 1, 
when its associated function f(z) has bounded variation there and 4/z f(z) 
is summable. 

It should finally be remarked that the results of the paper hold as 
they stand, not only for all values of m>0, but also for m >—4, and 
even for all values of m greater than —1, provided slight modifications be 
made, involving a change in the condition of summability in the neigh- 
bourhood of the origin of the function f(z) associated with the series.

6. The preceding theorem is important in connexion with the Cesaro 
summation of Bessel series, in virtue of the following theorem respecting 
the Cesaro convergence of Restricted Fourier Series :— 


THeorem.—The convergence (Ck) of a Restricted Fourier Series at any 
point, internal to the interval (—7 <a < 7), depends only on the nature 
of the associated function in the neighbourhood of the point, provided only 
the typical term tends to zero when divided by the k-th power of the tm 
teger expressing its place in the series.* 


For the proof of this theorem I refer to a forthcoming paper to be 
published elsewhere, as I do not propose to extend the length of the pre- 
sent communication by entering into a discussion of Cesaro convergence. 
It seems, however, relevant to the matter in hand to state and prove the 
above theorem respecting the convergence to zero of the typical Bessel 
term.

It follows that 2A4,J,(k,z) converges, or diverges, according as this 
R. F. series does so. Moreover the relations (21) and (29) are identities 
when we replace each of the infinite series involved by the corresponding 
n-th partial summations, so that, even when the R. F. series and the 
Bessel series do not converge, their oscillation properties are identical, 
and in particular uniform convergence or oscillation, or bounded conver- 
gence or oscillation, of the one series entails the same property in the 
other. Also term-by-term integration properties of the one series may 
be immediately inferred for one of the series if they are known to hold 
for the other.

31. Using this result in place of the corresponding theorem in the 
theory of R. F. series, the theorem of § 15 follows as before, m being now 
not necessarily rational. 

Thus the fundamental theorem of § 15 has been established in all its 
generality. 

More accurately what we have proved may be stated in the following 
TOD = 


Turorem.—The upper and lower functions of the Bessel series of f(x), 
at a point x internal to the interval O <a <1, are equal to those of the 
Fourier series of f(x), and the corresponding partial summations differ 
only by a quantity which tends uniformly to zero, provided the individual 
terms of the Bessel serves tend to zero.

33. The following extension of the main theorem is important :— 


Cor. 2.—The theorem is still true for a series of Bessel functions of 
the form (1), whose individual terms tend to zero, that is for any series

provided in some interval containing the point, or points, under considera-

tegral. 


In fact the condition (4), which makes our series in § 15 a Bessel 
series, nowhere enters into the proof itself of the main theorem, except 
tacitly, in quoting the theorem that the second integrated series converges 
to a repeated integral. 

Hence, as in Cor. 1, the first integrated series of such a series as we 
here contemplate converges to an integral. 

The function associated with the series of Bessel functions will then 
be the differential coefficient of the sum-function of the imtegrated series, 
in the interval under consideration in which this latter function vs an 
untegral. 

It is evidently sufficient for the fulfilment of the conditions of the 
above theorem, if tA Oy oe, 


and the integrated series converges uniformly to an integral. 


34. It is unnecessary to enumerate all the results for Bessel series and 
series of Bessel functions which can now be transferred wholesale from 
the theory of Fourier series to that of these series. These theorems in- 
clude, beside sufficient conditions for convergence, uniform convergence or 
oscillation, bounded convergence or oscillation, and so forth, as well as 
for Cesaro convergence, all theorems involving term-by-term integration 
when multiplied by suitable functions, provided we remain in the interior 
of the completely open interval (0, 1). 

As an example we may quote one of the simplest of those theorems 
which are an immediate consequence of the main theorem :— 


The Bessel serves of a function which, in an interval (a, 8), is a con- 
tinuous function of bounded variation converges uniformly to that fune- 
tion in (a, B); in particular this is the case in any interval (a, B) in 
which the function is an integral. 


Hence we have the following important result :— 


THEorEM.—If f(x) is, in a neighbourhood surrounding the point x, the 
integral of a function whose Fourier series converges at that point, e.g. if 
it is the integral of a continuous function of bounded variation in such a 
neighbourhood, then the derived series of the Bessel series of f(x) will con- 
verge at the point in question to the value f' (x).

INTRODUCTION AND SUMMARY. 


1.1. Our main object in this paper is to obtain as far-reaching a 
generalisation as possible of “‘Tauber’s Theorem”’, the well-known con- 
verse of Abel’s famous theorem concerning power-series. It will be 
necessary to give a rapid summary of the results already known; and we 
can do this most shortly and clearly if we begin with a few definitions of 
a verbal character. 

We shall always denote the power-series in question by 2a,z” or by 
S, and its sum by f(x). We suppose that the radius of convergence of 
S is unity, and that the point on the circle of convergence which is in 
question is the point  =1. ‘The series 2a, we shall call A, and we shall 
also use A to denote its sum, when it is convergent. 

We shall use (ix), (Z), (O), and (0) as abbreviations for the propositions :

L. (A) and (0) imply (K) whenever C is a regular path. 
As a corollary we have 
M. (L) and (0) umply (K) whenever C is a regular path. 


This theorem includes D and E as special cases. We also proved* the 
direct converse of L, viz. 


N. (A) and (0) vmply (A) whenever C is a regular path. 


This theorem becomes untrue if (A) is replaced by (Z). It is an 
“Abelian ” theorem, but differs fundamentally from the ordinary Abelian 
theorems A and B in that its truth depends upon a condition such as 
occurs in the ‘“‘ Tauberian”’ theorems. It is also unlike all the theorems 
which precede in being reversible: and, on combining it with L, we obtain 


0. If A satisfies (0), then the necessary and sufficient condition for its 
convergence 1s that (A) should be true when x tends to 1, ether along any 
regular path, or along all. 


1.4. We begin our new investigations by a direct extension of F to a 
regular Stolz-path, viz. 


P. (L) and (O) imply (K) when C is a regular Stolz-path. 


This theorem is included in others which come after and are proved 
in a quite different way. But the method we use (in 2.1) seems to us of 
considerable interest in itself. 

In 2.2 and the succeeding paragraphs we attack our main problem. 
Our object is to generalise L, (i) by replacing (0) by (O), and (ii) by getting 
rid of the restriction that C is a regular path ;'and the result is 


Q. (A) and (O) imply (K), for any path C. 
When C is regular, we can deduce as a corollary 


RB. (L) and (O) imply (K) whenever C is a regular path. 


* The proof of this theorem (Theorem 50) is not stated explicitly, but is virtually con-

s. (L) and (O) imply (K) whenever C is a Stolz-path. 


But we cannot here get rid of all restrictions upon C. 
In 8 we proceed to the corresponding Abelian theorems, and generalise 
N by proving 


T. (K) and (O) imply (A), for any path C. 
And by combining g and T, we obtain 


u. If A satisfies (O), then the necessary and sufficient condition for 
its convergence is that (A) should be true when « tends to 1, etther along 
any path C, or along all. 


This theorem affords the complete generalisation of O in each of the 
desired directions, and is far more comprehensive than any known 
theorem of its kind. 

There are but few questions which remain to be answered. There is 
one to which we have already alluded and are unable to answer, namely 
whether R (or S) is true without any restriction on C. The others are 
connected with the question whether T is the ‘“‘ best possible’ theorem of 
its kind. We prove 


Vv. (K) and (O) do not necessarily vmply (L) for all paths C: 


so that the (A) of © certainly cannot be replaced by (ZL). And almost the 
same example suffices to prove 


Ww. (K) does not necessarily imply (A) for all paths C. 


Thus 7' certainly becomes untrue if the condition (OQ) is simply omitted. 
But it is desirable to prove more, viz. that (O) cannot be replaced by any 
less restrictive condition of the type which occurs in K, and we have not 
yet succeeded in establishing this by means of an example. If we could 
do this, and also remove the restriction on C in & (or S$), we could fairly 
claim that our problem had been completely solved.

TurorEM R.—If (QO) is satisfied, and f(z) >A when x1 along 
some regular path C, then Xa, converges to the sum A. 


This theorem of course includes P as a special case. It is in the most 


220 Mr. G. H. Harpy and Mr. J. EK. Lirrnewoop (Dec. 6, 


essential respects as complete a generalisation of F as could be desired, 
for, although it involves a considerable limitation as to the nature of C, 
there is no limitation at all on the contact of C with the circle. The con-_ 
tact may be as close as we please, or C may be the circle itself: and it is 
questions of contact rather than of regularity which are of the first interest 
in these investigations. . 

It is, however, natural to suppose that mo limitation on C is necessary ; 
and, if this be so, it is very desirable that it should be proved. It would 
seem, however, that some important change in our argument would be 
needed for such a proof; for, unless C is subject to considerable restric- 
tions, it is not possible to deduce the behaviour of ® from that of f by 
means of its representation as an integral taken along -C. 

It is interesting to observe that, if we limit C to be a Stolz-path, we 
can get rid of the restriction ‘that it is tobe regular. We shall only sketch 
the proof, the general lines of which are as follows. We draw two chords 
of the circle through « = 1, including C between them, and we consider 
the region 7’ formed by points which lie between these chords and within 
a certain distance of «= 1. 

By a slight modification of the proof of Theorem E given by Landau, 
we can prove that s, is bounded, and so that f(z) is bounded in 7.* And 
by an adaptation of the arguments used in $§ 2.38-4, we can show that f 
tends to a limit when «> 1 along any regular Stolz-path inside 7. If 
the real axis satisfies this condition, we can appeal to Theorem F; if not, 
to Theorem P or R. In any case, we obtain 


THrorem 8.—If (O) 2s satisfied and f(z)> A when «1 along some 
Stolz-path C, then Xa, converges to the sum A.

3.5. We can easily adapt the example by which we proved Theorem Vv,

THEOREM W.—It is possible to find a convergent series La, and a 
regular path C, so that (x) does not tend to a limit when « tends to 1 
along C. 


We take An = (N+1) da, 


where b, is the a of the last paragraph. The argument by which we 
proved 2b, convergent is sufficient to establish the convergence of Dapy.* 
1 


Also P(z) = ie ZR Lae 


B—«xg (cz) 
era 
where B = Xb, and g(z) = 2b, 2". And as g(x) does not tend to a limit, 
(x) does not do so. 

This proves the theorem. It will be observed that there is a great 
deal to spare in the conclusion: we have proved, in fact, that (x) assumes 
values of order greater than that of 1/|1—z|. The fact is that it ought 
to be possible to prove that series exist which satisfy the conditions of 
Theorem W and whose coefficients are of order ¢(n)/n, where ¢(n) is any 
function which tends to infinity with ». Such an example would (in 
conjunction with Theorem V) prove that Theorem T is a best possible 
theorem in the same sense as (e.g.) Theorem F, and that neither hypo- 
theses nor conclusion can be improved upon. We have no doubt that 
this ig true, but we have not succeeded in finding an example to prove 
our point. In our last example the order of a, is 1/logn, which is far 
from the limit desired. It is therefore not surprising that we should 
find that our argument carries us some distance over the mark. 

At any rate, however, Theorem W is enough to show that, in Theorem 
T, some condition beyond that of mere convergence is essential.

TueoreM Z.—The Fourier series of a function f(0), bounded in the 
neighbourhood of the particular value of 0 under consideration, is either 
summable by Cesaro means of arbitrarily small positive order, or summable 
by no Cesaro mean of any order. The necessary and sufficient condition 
that it should be summable is expressed by the condition (4.18). 


The proof of this theorem would, however, carry us too far from the 
proper subject of the paper.*

ON THE CONTINUED FRACTIONS CONNECTED WITH THE 
HYPERGEOMETRIC EQUATION 


By ¥%. Linpsay Ince. 


[Received April 24th, 1918.—Read May 9th, 1918.] 


1. Introductory Remarks. 


The infinite continued fractions dealt with in the present paper differ 
radically from those which most frequently occur in analysis. The latter 
are usually of the form 


1+1+ 


UyZ AgZ- Agz 
1 +... ad inf., 
or of the form 
biz bez? b, 2" 


(2t+d, + cozt+d, +e3z+d, +... ad inf., 


or again of Stieltjes’ form 


pias he Ail ak 
€1:2 + & tes2 + e, +... ad inf. 


These three forms of continued fraction may readily be transformed 
into one another ; it is therefore sufficient to consider only the first, which 
is the form into which a power series is most easily converted.* It has 
been studied, from the point of view of function theory, by Pringsheim 
and by Van Vleck,+ who found that if the coefficients a,, a), ... (which 
may be complex numbers) tend to a limit k (real or complex), the con- 
tinued fraction will converge and represent an analytic function over the 
whole of the z-plane, with the exception of (1) a rectilinear cut drawn 
from the point z =—1/4k to infinity in the direction away from the 


* Heilermann, Crelle, Vol. 33 (1845), p. 174; Muir, Proc. London Math. Soc., Vol. 7 
(1876), p. 112. 
+ See especially Trans. Amer. Math. Soc., Vol. 5 (1904), p. 258. 


1918.] ConrINUED FRACTIONS AND THE HYPERGEOMETRIC EQUATION. 237 


origin, and (2) certain isolated points.* The cut in question does not 
divide the plane into two or more distinct regions, and the same holds for 
the other two continued fractions into which the first may be transformed. 

The case of the continued fractions connected with the hypergeometric 
equation will, as we have said, be found to differ from the above. The 
important point of difference is that in their case there exists, for each 
continued fraction, a closed cut which divides the z-plane into distinct 
regions. Any one continued fraction represents different analytic func- 
tions on opposite sides of the associated cut; in other words, the cuts 
form impassable barriers, heyond which the continued fraction ceases to 
represent one analytic function, but may represent another. 

There are nevertheless other, though analogous, forms of continued 
fractions associated with the hypergeometric equation which may have 
different cuts. It results that when one continued fraction ceases to 
represent a certain function, it may be possible to find another continued 
fraction which will continue to do so. We have thus a kind of analytical 
continuation by means of which we are able, to some extent, to carry the 
representation of a function over the barrier. We shall find, however, 
that in no case can a function be represented over the entire plane by 
continued fractions of the type considered.

The foregoing procedure is essentially due to Perron, who, however, 
only showed that, when R(z) <4, the continued fraction S, represents the 
logarithmic derivate of F(a, G; y; 2. That this continued fraction 
represents, when R(z) > 4, quite another function does not appear to 
have been so far noted. This is indeed the most striking property of this 
continued fraction and of the others to be considered in § 8, and dis- 
tinguishes them in a marked manner from the continued fraction of 
Heilermann’s form. Some of the consequences of this essential peculiarity 
of the continued fractions associated with the hypergeometric equation will 
be discussed in the following sections (§§ 8, 4).

4. Relations existing between the Twelve Continued Fractions at 
Different Parts of the z-Plane. 


It is well known that the 24 fundamental hypergeometric functions 
| are not all independent. They can ‘in fact be grouped into six sets of 
| four, such that the members of each set are equal to or constant mul- 
Pee . 


244 Dr. E. L. Ince [May 9, 


tiples of one another. In like manner the corresponding logarithmic 
derivates with respect to z can be grouped into six sets each comprising 
four equal members. We have; in fact, the following relations ‘s

The twelve continued fractions we have obtained converge over the 
whole of the z-plane with the exception of certain isolated points which 
represent the zeros of the solution whose logarithmic derivate is in 
question, and with the exception of the three closed cuts R(z) = 3, 
{z—1|=1 and |z|=1. Each continued fraction ceases to converge on 
one only of these cuts and represents on one side of that cut a certain 
one of the functions H, and on the other side a different function H. 
The three cuts divide the plane into six distinct regions A, B, C, D, E, 
F (Fig. 1), within each of which every one of the twelve continued

fractions represents a definite function, and in each of which the con-—

Here we notice that no equation between any two of the twelve con- 
tinued fractions holds throughout the whole plane, but each continued 
fraction is equal, in two of the regions, to three other continued fractions, 
and in the four other regions to one other continued fraction. Further, 


no function H is represented over the whole plane by means of the con- 
tinued fractions, but each H is represented in four regions only. 


5. Logarithmic Cases. 


Exceptional cases arise in three distinct circumstances, viz., y = 1, 
y=at+, and a=. Similar phenomena arise in all three cases, so 
that it will be sufficient to consider only the case of y=1. The two 
solutions

The continued fraction S, now degenerates into S,, S, into S,, 5, into 
Sy, and Sj. into Sy. The function H, also becomes H,. Thus in this 
case the continued fraction obtained by repeated integration (S,) leads to 
the same solution as that got by repeated differentiation (Sj), so that in 
the logarithmic case we do not arrive at a complete solution of the equa- 
tion by the use of continued fractions. This failure to arrive at a com- 
plete solution is met with in the case of Legendre’s equation.

When R(z)>0, this continued fraction converges to the logarithmic 
derivate of the solution corresponding to the exponent 0 at the singularity 
z2=-+1, and when R(z) <0 to the logarithmic derivate of the solution 
corresponding to the exponent O at the singularity z=—1. It so 
happens, as is revealed by the symmetry of the P-function, that these 
two solutions are identical with one another, so that the continued frac- 
tion (8) represents a certain odd function over the whole of the z-plane 
with the exception of the imaginary axis. 

These results show us that in Region I (Fig. 2), continued fraction (8) 
is 2z times continued fraction (6). In Region II, (8) retains the signifi- 
cance it had in I, but (6) loses this significance, and there represents the 
logarithmic derivate of a solution appropriate to the neighbourhood of the 
origin. 

We might, as before, deduce from each of the two differential equa- 
tions (4) and (7), twelve continued fractions, and then proceed to set up 
relations between them.

ON HELLINGER’S INTEGRALS 


By K. W. Hopson. 
[Received June 1st, 1918.—Read June\13th, 1918.] 


In connection with the theory of quadratic forms involving an infinite 
number of variables, which theory is of fundamental importance in rela- 
tion to integral equations, certain limits occur for the representation of 
which Hellinger used the notation of integration. These integrals, re- 
garded by Hellinger as a new species of limit, were shewn by Hahn to be 
reducible to the ordinary integrals defined in the manner introduced into 
analysis by Lebesgue. In the present communication this reduction is 
established by a method which will, I think, be found to be appreciably 
simpler than that employed by Hahn. An extension is also given to a 
wider class of cases which includes the limits investigated by Hellinger 
and Hahn as a special case. 


1. Let us suppose an interval (a, 6) to be divided into a number of 
parts (a, x), (1, LZ), ---, (@n-1, 6), the greatest of which is 6; let a 
further division of (a, b) be made by dividing some or all of the above 
parts by the introduction of fresh points of division. Let it be supposed 
that a system of successive sub-divisions of the interval (a, b) is defined 
by means of an indefinite continuation of the process. At the m-th stage 
of the process, let 6") be the greatest of the intervals into which (a, 0) 
has been divided. If the system of successive sub-divisions be such that 
6” converges to zero as m~®, the system will be spoken of as a con- 
vergent system of successive sub-divisions of the interval (@, b). Such 
convergent systems are employed in the definition of the Riemann integral 


of a limited function. It is convenient to denote the intervals into which 


(a,b) is divided at the m-th stage by 6{”, 62”, ..., 61; 


ber of such intervals, and n,~© as m~o. In what follows it will 
frequently be convenient to leave out m, and to denote the intervals by

A DETAIL IN CONFORMAL REPRESENTATION 


By J. HopaxKinson. 


[Received October 14th, 1918.—Read November 14th, 1918.] 


I. Introductory. 


The writer has discussed certain aspects of the conformal representa- 
tion of a curvilinear triangle on a previous occasion. It was then shown 
with regard to those triangles whose sides have a real common orthogonal 
circle that, in order that the function z, which effects the representation 
upon a half plane of a curvilinear triangle in the w plane, may have a 
finite number of branches, the triangle must be a reduced triangle; and 
further that it must be built up of symmetrical repetitions of a triangle 
whose angles are 7/2, 7/J, z/m, where J, m are integers. It was also shown 
how in any particular case the possibility or otherwise of such a composi- 
tion can be determined by a finite number of tests.* 

It will now be shown that the total number of such compositions 
is ten. 

The method employed can be applied equally to the cases of those tri- 
angles whose sides are rectilinear or have an imaginary common ortho- 
gonal circle. These cases, however, have been fully discussed otherwise. + 
I have also discussed the case in which one or more of the angles of the 
triangle is zero. 


Il. The Triangle with Finite Angles. 


Suppose we have a fundamental triangle whose angles are 7/2, z/p, 
a/q. The sum of the angles of any composite triangle built up of sym- 
metrical repetitions of this fundamental triangle is

These solutions however do not all lead to possible composite triangles, 
while some lead to two distinct triangles. This is because we have as yet 
taken no account of the manner in which a is split up into its two parts 
ad, and 2a. 

The inquiry however is unnecessary, for we have figures which decide 
the possibility of composition or otherwise upon inspection.*

‘The roughest of figures is sufficient to demonstrate the impossibility of 
the composition of a. triangle (7/9, 7/9, z/9), by means of twelve repeti- 
tions of,a triangle (7/2, 7/8, 7/9). For, since ¢ =1, nine repetitions of 
the fundamental triangle have a common vertex on one of the sides of the 
attempted composite triangle. These form a hexagon and it is manifestly 
impossible to convert this into a triangle by adding three more repetitions. 

In the same way we satisfy ourselves of the impossibility of building 
up the triangle (7/8, 7/8, 7/8) by means of ten repetitions of the triangle 
(1/2, 1/8, 7/8). 

In the other case in which the triangle (7/2, 7/8, 7/8) occurs, by 
means of six repetitions we build up the triangle (7/2, 7/8, 7/8). This 
being itself repeated we get the triangle (7/4, 7/8, 7/8). 

From the figures, then, and these last considerations we arrive at the 
following possible compositions :

Certain of the results which appear are, in a sense, accidental. Thus

from the triangle (7/2, 7/4, 7/q), we obtain the triangle (7/2, 7/q, 7/¢) ; 
and this triangle also being right-angled is capable of being used for 
further composition by means of which we get the triangle (27/q, z/q, 7/q)- 
Similarly from the triangle (7/2, 7/8, 7/8), we obtained the triangle 
(7/2, 7/8, 7/8) which again led to the triangle (7/4, 7/8, 7/8). These 
appeared in the solution of the indeterminate equations. 

In a similar manner we sometimes find that the sets of triangles. 
derived from two fundamental triangles have a triangle in common. 

Thus the set derived from the triangle (7/2, 7/3, 7/9) is

II. The Triangle with some of its Angles Zero. 


If we apply the same methods to the conformal representation of tri- 
angles with zero angles our equations become 


2a+l=k, pb+m=k, n=k, 
atb=k-—-1. 


The number ¢ disappears, for we cannot build up an angle z or 27 by 
means of a finite number of repetitions of zero. The possible solutions 
are the same as those of the general case in which q has an arbitrary 
value; in this instance q is made infinite. 

The number of triangles which have what we called an accidental 
connection is considerable; for, retaining only those which give rise to

The corresponding triangle functions can then all be expressed in terms 
of that derived from the triangle (0, 0, 0). This is, in fact, reflected in 
many known results in the theory of Elliptic Modular Functions. 

For example, the function of the triangle (0, 0, 0) in its simplest form

As the substitutions for which a triangle function remains unchanged are 
obtained by successive inversions in two sides of the triangle, this method 
provides a simple means of determining those substitutions.

DETERMINANTAL SYSTEMS OF CO-APOLAR TRIADS ON A 
CUBIC CURVE 


By Wituram P. Mitne, M.A., D.Se. 


[Received May 7th, 1918.—Read May 9th, 1918.] 


1. Introduction. 


In the Proc. London Math. Soc., Ser. 2, Vol. 15, pp. 809-818, I 
studied in some detail the grouping of the triads of points co-apolar with 
a given triad on a cubic curve. I found that if ABC be an assigned 
“apolar triangle,’ and if P be any point chosen arbitrarily on the cubic 
curve, then two complementary dyads of points Q,R; and Q;R, can be 
found such that P,Q@.R3; and P,Q;R, are each co-apolar with ABC. If, 
thereafter, any other point of those obtained above such as Q, be taken, 
the dyad complementary to Q,; other than P,R; may be denoted by P;R,, 
and so on, with the other points. Proceeding in this way, it was shewn 
that a closed system of nine points was obtained, namely : 


Qi Qs Qa» 
fi, Ry Rs, 


such that if any one of the six triads defined by the terms of the expan- 
sion of the above determinantal form were taken, each triad so obtained 
would be co-apolar with ABC. Such a system of nine points was called 
a “determinantal system.” The configuration is in its essence a sym- 
metrical one, but the method of obtaining it in the above paper gave no 
clue as to the symmetry of its form, nor any convenient means of visual- 
izing its structure as a geometrical unit. The purpose of the present 
communication is to establish the symmetry of the configuration. 


2. The Direct Proposition. 


Let UVW and XYZ be any two coplanar triangles, and let us consider 
the pencil of cubic curves circumscribing these two triangles and apolar 


1918.] DererMinantaL SYSTEMS OF CO-APOLAR TRIADS ON A CUBIC CURVE. 275 


to them. This pencil of cubics will have in common three other points 
ABC, which we shall refer to as the triangle “ complementary ” to UVW 
and XYZ. It was shewn in the Proceedings of the Edinburgh Mathe- 
matical Society, Vol. 30, Session 1911-12, that every member of the 
pencil of cubics is apolar to the complementary triangle ABC. It will 
now be proved that every member of the pencil of cubic curves circwm- 
scribing two triangles and apolar to them cuts the nine lines joining the 


vertices of these triangles in a determinantal system of points co-apolar 
with the complementary triangle. 


Let a cubic of the pencil cut the straight lines 


OX Wie. SV Z, 
VY, SUZ, WX, 


in the respective points, 


Ly P, Ps, 
Q) Qs Qs; 
fi, fk, Ry 


Let us consider first of all the nodal members of the pencil. It was 
shewn in the Proc. London Math. Soc., Ser. 2, Vol. 9, pp. 235-248, that 
the necessary and sufficient condition that two apolar triads on a nodal 
cubic be co-apolar is that the pencils subtended by them at the node shall 
be apolar. It was also shewn in the above-mentioned paper in the Proc. 
Edin. Math. Soc., that the canonical form for three complementary 
apolar triangles on the nodal cubic x= 60, y = 6t, 7 = —(1+¢#*) can 
be taken to be 


ABC = #+38p?+3qi+pq = 0, (1) 
; XYZ = 4+38r?+8st+rs = 0, 2) 
UVW = pgrst?+8qst?+3pri+1 = 0. (3) 


If now we project the triad XYZ through the point t, of the nodal 
cubic on to the nodal cubic again, so as to obtain the triad

we see that the apolar triads (1) and (4) subtend pencils at the node which 
are apolar to one another, if

76 Dr. W. P. Mitne [May 9,. 


Hence we obtain the following result :— 


If a nodal cubic circumscribe the two triangles ABC, XYZ and be 
apolar to them, and if XYZ be projected through any one of the vertices 
of the complementary triangle UVW on to the cubic again, the apolar 
triangle thus obtained ts co-apolar with ABC. 


If U be the vertex chosen through which the projection is to take 
place, we have thus shewn that the triangle P,;Q,R3 1s co-apolar with 
ABO. Similarly with the other members of the determinantal configura- 
tion defined by the intersections of the nodal cubic with the lines joining 
the vertices of the triangles XYZ, UVW. ‘The required theorem has 
therefore been proved for the nodal members of the pencil of cubics. We 
proceed to establish the general result for those members of the pencil 
which do not possess singular points. 


°C 


In the above figure, let U be one of the vertices of the triangle com- 
plementary to ABC, XYZ. Let R, be any point on UY, and let the cubic 
circumscribing ABC, XYZ and apolar to each of them which passes 
through f;, cut UZ. in @,. Then plainly a one-to-one algebraic corre- 
spondence exists between R; and Q». Again, let Q3 be taken on UZ, so 
that X[U, Rs, Q2] is apolar to X[ABC]. It is plain, once more, that a 


a ee pee 


1918.] DererMINaNnraL SYSTHMS OF CO-APOLAR TRIADS ON A CUBIC CURVE. 277 


one-to-one algebraic correspondence exists between Ry and @Q3. But it 
has been shewn above that in the case of the twelve nodal eubies of the 
pencil, @, and @3 coincide. Hence Q, and Qi must coincide for every 
member of the pencil. 

We thus have the following result :— 


If U be one of the vertices of the triangle complementary to ABO, 
XYZ, and if a cubic circumscribing ABC, XYZ, and apolar to each of 
them, cut UX, UY, UZ in P,R,Q, respectively, then P,R3Q. subtends at 
X a pencil of lines whichis apolar to X[ABC]. Similarly for Y and Z. 


But since U lies on the “apolar locus” of ABC, XYZ, we see that 
U[ABC] is apolar to U[XYZ], «.e. to U[P;R;Qs]. Hence the “ apolar 
locus” of ABC, P,Q.R3 must pass through the points X, Y, Z, U, and 
therefore have ten points in common with the above cubic circumscribing 
ABC, XYZ, and apolar to each of them, and passing through P,Q, R3. 
The two cubics must therefore coincide. We see therefore with respect 
to the cubic of the above pencil under consideration that P,Q@,R3 is a 
triangle co-apolar with ABC. The final result is now established : 


The lines jouning the vertices of any two apolar triangles on a cubic 
curve cut the curve again im nine points forming a determinantal system, 
co-apolar with the complementary triangle of the given apolar triangles. 


a 


3. The Converse Proposition. 
We next proceed to establish the converse : 


If ABC be a given apolar triad and | PQR | a definite determinantal 
system of co-apolar triads, then | PQR | can in a singly-infimte number of 
ways be regarded as the further intersections of the cubic curve with the 
Unes joining the vertices of two apolar triangles XYZ, UVW complemen- 
tary with ABC. 


Let U be any point on the cubic curve and let us join U to the three 
points P,, Qz, R3, so that UP,, URs, UQ, cut the cubic again in X, Y, Z 
respectively. Then, since P,Q.R; is an apolar triangle, we know that 
XYZ is an apolar triangle. Further, since ULXYZ] is apolar to U[ABC], 
we see that U is one of the vertices of the triangle complementary to 
ABC, XYZ. Let V, W be the other two vertices of the triangle .comple- 
mentary to ABC, XYZ. Let XV, XW meet the curve again in f%, Qs. 
Then, since X[UVW] is apolar to XLABC], we see from § 2 that P,, Re, 
Qs are co-apolar with ABC. Thus the complementary dyads of P; are 
Qo, R3, and Q3, R2 (according to the notation of the Proc. London Math. 


278 Dr. W. P. Minne [May 9, 


Soc., Ser. 2, Vol. 15, pp. 809-813). Hence Qs, R; must coincide with 
Qs, Ry of the determinantal system |PQR|. Proceeding in this way, we 
see that all the lines joining pairs of vertices of XYZ and UVW must 
pass each through a member of the determinantal system | PQR|. 
Moreover, we chose U arbitrarily on the cubic curve, and thence ob- 
tained the other five points XYZ, VW uniquely. Hence, since U can 
be chosen in a singly-infinite number of ways, we see that the given 
determinantal system can be regarded as being deduced from a singly- 
infinite system of pairs of apolar triangles. 


4. Apolar Triangles on a Conic and Tangents to the Cayleyan. 


The case when the two fundamental apolar triangles XYZ, UV W lie 
on a conic is of considerable geometrical interest. In this case the com- 
plementary triad ABC consists of three collinear points lying on the line 
joiing the poles of the conic (regarded as a polar conic) with respect to 
the triangles XYZ, UVW (regarded as cubic curves), while the tangents 
at A, B, C to any cubic of the pencil circumscribing XYZ, UVW and 
apolar to them are concurrent. Moreover, the triads P,P, Ps3, Q1Qs Qs; 
f,R,R; consist each of three collinear points, as do also the triads 
P,Q: Fy, PoQofo, P3Q3h3. In fact, we obtain the determinantal system 
_ dealt with in the Proc. London Math. Soc., Ser. 2, Vol. 9, pp. 285-243, in 
which the given cubic is shewn to pass through the nine intersections of 
three concurrent lines with three other concurrent lines, it being further 
shewn that the points of concurrency are *‘ corresponding points’’ on the 
Hessian curve. Hence in the above configuration, P,P, P3, Q:Q2Qs3; 
f, RR, are straight lines passing through one Hessian point of the 
collinear triad ABC, while P,Q,A,, PyQ.Ro, P3Q3Rh3 pass through the 
other. : 

We may proceed further in the search for geometrical properties. It is. 
known that an apolar triad becomes a tangent to the Cayleyan if the three 
points constituting the triad are collinear. Hence we may take XYZ, 
UVW to be any two tangents m,n to the Cayleyan. The complementary 
triad ABC will lie on a line / which is also a tangent to the Cayleyan. 
Given m and n, it is easy to construct J. For, it was shewn in the 
Proc. Edin. Math. Soc., in the paper quoted above, that the conic 
XYZUVW of last paragraph harmonically separates the Hessian points 
of the triad ABC. Hence, the conic composed of J, m must harmonically 
separate the Hessian points of UVW, 7.e. the line 7 is the join of the 
harmonic conjugates of the point mn with respect to the Hessian points 
of XYZ and UVW. We thus see that the lines joining the points of the 


1918.] DereRMINaNnraL SYSTEMS OF CO-APOLAR TRIADS ON A CUBIC CURVE. 279 


collinear triads lying on the lines m and n cut the cubic again in nine 
points which lie three by three on six straight lines that pass through 
one or other of two “ corresponding points”’ on the Hessian of the given 
cubic curve, whose position is defined on the line 7 in the way stated 
above. 

If m and n together constitute a polar-conic of the given cubic, and 
m', n' be the polar-conie of the point mn, it is easy to shew that the line 
1 is in this case that line which with the join of mn and m’'n! constitutes 
a polar-conic of the original cubic, since every polar-conic cuts the join of 


a pair of “corresponding points” harmonically. We thus obtain the 
following theorem :— 


If a degenerate polar-conic of the original cubic curve consist of the 
lines m and n, and if m', n' be the polar conic of the point mn, the nine 
lines joining the points of section of m and n with the cubic cut the curve 
again in nine points which lie three by three on six lines passing through 
one or other of the pair of “corresponding points” in which the line l 
cuts the Hessian curve, where lis the line which together with the jon of 
mn and m'n' constitutes a degenerate polar-conic of the original cubic. 


Symmetry shews that the joins of the points of section of m', n' with 
the cubic curve pass through one or other of the same pair of “ correspond- 
ing points”? on the Hessian curve. 


Many other theorems of a like nature might be stated by taking p.r- 
ticular positions for two tangents to the Cayleyan. 

I take the opportunity of expressing my indebtedness to Dr. William 
L. Marr, of Aberdeen, a communication from whom first suggested the 
mode of treatment in the present paper.

THE ELECTROMAGNETIC PROPERTIES OF COILS OF 
CERTAIN FORMS 


By A. Youne. 
[Read May 9th, 1918.—Received September 20th, 1918. ] 


Tus magnetic potential of a closed circuit at any point outside the 
circuit is measured by wi, where » is the solid angle subtended by the circuit 
at the point, and 7 is the strength of the current. The magnetic potential 
of a coil can be obtained by summing the expressions w2 for each turn. 

The induction parallel to the axis of a coil at any point is, of course, 
the ratio of the difference between the potentials at two neighbouring 
points—such that the straight line joining them is parallel to the axis— 
to the distance between the points. The effect would be the same if we 
took the difference between the potentials of two coils at the same point, 
tbe one coil being obtained from the other by a small displacement parallel 
to the axis. ‘Thus, for a single layer coil of » turns to the centimetre, 
nQi is the magnetic induction parallel to the axis at a point, where ( is 
the solid angle subtended by the coil itself at the point. 

In what follows coils are considered in which each turn is in the form 
of a rectangle. The solid angle subtended by a rectangle at any point 
may be represented as the inverse tangent of a certain ratio. Hence is 
deduced by integration the magnetic potential of such a coil. The coil is 
looked upon as an infinitely thin current sheet. 

A further double integration enables either the self induction of the 
coil to be written down, or else the mutual induction of two such eoils 
which are such that the three principal planes are parallel ; 2.e. the planes 
at right angles to the axis and those through the axis at right angles to 
the sides of the rectangular turns. 

In $8 the potential is obtained for a coil of the same form but of 
finite thickness, and thence by double integration the mutual induction of 
two such coils whose axes and sides are parallel. 

In § 4 the self induction of this coil is given. 

In §5 the question of the looseness of the windings is taken into 


1918.] ELECTROMAGNETIC PROPERTIES OF COILS OF CERTAIN FORMS. 281 


account, and in the remaining paragraphs the discussion of § 5 is applied 
to the cases considered.

may consider the coil as equivalent to an infinitely thin current sheet.

axes of x and y parallel to the sides 2a, 2b respectively of the coil. The 
axes are so chosen that they form a right handed system. Now the solid 
angle subtended by a rectangle ABCD at any point O on the straight line

7. The mutual inductance of two coils whose axes and plane sides are

divergent series and infinities may be avoided too, for the case when one 
coil partly encloses the other, by supposing the magnetic force due to the 
outer coil linked with the current in the inner coil, and taking the summa- 
tions in the order due to this arrangement.

DIFFRACTION OF WAVES BY A WEDGE OF ANY ANGLE. “91 


DIFFRACTION OF WAVES BY A WEDGE OF ANY ANGLE 


By H. 8. Carstaw. 


[Received October 17th, 1916.—In Revised form December 16th, 1918.— 
Read February 18th, 1919.*] 


Tue solution of the problem of diffraction by a wedge of any angle in 
the two dimensional cases of plane waves and a line-source, and the three 
dimensional case of a point-source, is due to Macdonald.t The case of a 
point-source has been treated differently by Bromwich.{ If I understand 
him aright, he believes that Sommerfeld’s methods are only of use in the 
case of the half-plane, or wedge of angle 27. As a matter of fact 
Sommerfeld himself showed how, by his methods, the solution could be 
obtained for a wedge of angle nz/m (m, nm being positive integers), a 
Rieraann’s surface of 2 sheets being used, and he pointed out that, when 
the angle of the wedge is not commensurable with 7, a Riemann’s surface 
of an infinite number of sheets can be employed in the same way.|| Re- 
cently Wiegrefe has given a very complete discussion of the diffraction of 
plane waves by a wedge of any angle by this method, and he has also 
given the formal solution for a line-source and point-source. In §§ 2, 8 
below, a statement of Sommerfeld’s method will be found. 

In this paper I also show that the three problems solved by 
Macdonald by his method of deducing the solution in series for the wave 
equation from the corresponding solution in series for the equation of 
potential can be treated by the modification of Sommerfeld’s work, em- 
ployed by me in dealing with Green’s functions for the Wedge of any

waves when the direction in which they are travelling is not perpendicular

ON NON-HARMONIC FouRIER SERIES. 307 


ON NON-HARMONIC FOURIER SERIES 


By W. H. Youne. 


[Received March 13th, 1918. — Read March 14th, 1918.] 


1. Following English usage, I have found it convenient to restrict the 
term Fourier series to those trigonometric series which proceed by cosines 
and sines of integral values of the variable, or variables, and have the 
further property that their coefficients are expressible by means of in- 
tegrals of the well-known kind, involving a function of the independent 
variable, or variables. I have, for example, not followed the usage 
adopted by Jordan, and other French writers, of employing the term to 
characterise analogous series of functions, other than cosnx and sin nz, 
such as Bessel functions. It seems to me, however, desirable to retain 
the term when the very slight change-—formally speaking—of substituting 
n+k for nis made. Though the series so obtained have not, however, 
as far as I know, been the subject of systematic study, and I have found 
it necessary to obtain among other things, the expressions for the co- 
efficients as integrals, such series naturally present themselves. Special 
examples of such series are indeed known.* I myself have been led to the 
study of these series naturally by the necessity of extending to the case 
when the order is irrational, properties of series of Bessel functions, which, 
in the rational case, I have been able to deduce from the theory of what I 
have called Restricted Fourier Series. Needless to say Series of Bessel 
Functions are not the only series whose study might be expected to 
. demonstrate the same need. 

In the researches in question, these non-harmonic trigonometrical 
series do not present themselves in what I propose to call the Fourier 
form, being of a more general type. Iteis evident, however, that a proper 
grasp of the whole class of trigonometrical series in question cannot be 
obtained without investigating the properties of the particular class in 
question. Indeed the behaviour of a Bessel series} at the further bound 


* See below, footnote to § 11. 
+ I distinguish between a series of Bessel functions 24,Jm (k,z) and the special case, 
Bessel series, when the coefficients A have the appropriate form. 


oe 


308 Pror. W. H. Youne {March 14, 


of the interval in which the expansion is valid, is most conveniently dis- 
cussed by reference to the behaviour of a non-harmonic Fourier series, 
restricted in character generally, at an extremity of the interval in which 
this series is defined. ore 

The paper falls naturally into three parts. The first of these, $$ 2-16, 
treats of the definition and summation of the series in question.

At an internal point of the interval (0, 27) these series are proved to 
have, apart from an additive term which tends uniformly to zero, the 
same expression for the »-th partial summation 


. 
Sn = o(1)+ +\' 4 { f(a+h+/f(c—b)} cosec dé sin(n+4)t dt, 
as the Fourier series of a function equal to f(z) at, and in the neighbour- 
hood of, the point x, and having any convenient values elsewhere. The 
upper and lower functions, and the modes of oscillation, are accordingly 
independent of the form of the associated function, except in an arbitrary 
small neighbourhood of the point « considered, and are the same as those 
of this auxiliary Fourier series. In particular, the conditions of conver- 
gence, and of uniform or bounded convergence, are the same for the non- 
harmonic Fourier series and the auxiliary harmonic Fourter serves. 

These results are still true at the end-points of the interval (0, 2z7), 
provided the function f(x), whose values have hitherto only been supposed 
known almost everywhere in the interval (0, 27), is supposed “‘ continued ” 
outside the interval in accordance with certain laws, which are different 
for the cosine and sine series. The law of ‘ continuation ”’ appropriate to 
the cosine is embodied in the formule : 


f® = f(-, eer 
31 fQr+d+fQr—2)} = f(t) cos Qkr. J 


1918. ] ON Non-wARMONIC FourIER SERIES. 309 


The law of ‘‘ continuation” appropriate to the sine series is as follows: 


3 f(Qr+t)—f2r—2t} = f(t) cos 2k. j 


These “ continuation ” formule play in our present theory the part of the 
periodicity in the case of harmonic series. 
The proof of these properties depends on certain fundamental lemmas, 


which, in our theory, take the place of the ordinary normal properties of 
the sine and cosine. These are 


2" sin Gen 
|, cos (n+ k) (2 —u) sin r+thudu=0O (rn) 
a =4+7sin2kr (r=n), 


vr and being integers, positive, negative, or zero. In particular it 
appears that, if a function is expressible as the sum of either a convergent 
non-harmonic Fourier cosine series, or the corresponding sine series, at a 
point z internal to the interval (0, 27), it is expressible by both these 
series ; but that this is not the case at the points 0 and 27, unless the 
value of the function at each of these points is zero. At the origin indeed 
a sine series, of course, converges to zero, and at 27 each term of a cosine 
series returns to its value at the origin, multiplied by cos 2k7. 

It follows from the theorem of Riemann-Lebesgue, that the coeflicients 
of a non-harmonie Fourier series tend to zero as we advance along the 
series. Hence the second integrated series necessarily converges uni- 
formly to a continuous function. We are thus able to show at once that 
the necessary and sufficient condition that a non-harmonic “?*’"” serves 
should have the Fourter form is that the second integrated series should 
converge to a second integral,* and the associated function is then the 
second differential coefficient of this second integral almost everywhere in 
(0, 27): also, if we prefer to utilise at once both types of series, that we 
may in this statement replace “ second” by “ first.” 

The first part of the paper terminates with some simple examples of 
non-harmonie Fourier cosine and sine series, the nature of whose conver- 
gence, uniform or bounded, is discussed. 

The object of the second part of the paper, §§ 17-25, is mainly to 
shew that there is no other way of obtaining the development of a given 
function in such a series as is here contemplated. For this and later 


* That is the integral of an integral.

purposes we require the generalisation of Riemann’s “ Three Theorems on 
Trigonometrical Series,” given in his Habilitationsschrift. 

Harnack’s theorem, too, as to certain conditions under which the co- 
efficients of a trigonometrieal series tend to zero is generalised so as to 
apply to non-harmonic series. In particular we have the result that, ¢f 
the points of non-convergence form a set which is at most of the jirst cate- 
gory in some interval, then the coefficients tend to zero. 

One of the most important consequences of these theorems is the re-

The main object of the third part of the paper (§§ 26 to end), is. to 
apply the considerations which precede to establish rigidly a statement 
which I made in a previous papert as to the behaviour of a Bessel series 
at the point z= 1. For this purpose it is convenient. to use the theory 
of restricted non-harmonic Fourier series in a slightly extended form. 
In the light of the continuation formuls this theory is found to apply not 
only when the interval of restriction belongs to the completely open in- 
terval (0, 27), but even when one of these points is an included end-point 
of the interval of restriction. 

In consequence we may now state that the conditions of convergence 
of a Bessel serves at any point of the half-open mterval (0< x2 <1) are 
the same as those of the Fourier series of a function equal to the asso- 
ciated function f(z) of the Bessel series in an arbitrary small neighbour- 
hood surrounding the point considered and having any convenient values 
elsewhere, provided the value assigned to f(z) at any point to the right of 
the point z = 2m rs defined as the same as that at the reflection of this 
pomt in z= Ar.

first derived series of the non-harmonic Fourier sine series of an integral

verges uniformly to that integral in the closed interval (0, 27), we see that 
in the preceding statement in ¢talics we may write cosine for sine. 

We may state these results otherwise by saying: The necessary and 
sufficient condition that a non-harmonic sine or cosine series should have 
the Fourier form is that the first integrated series should converge towards 
an wntegral in the closed interval (0, 27). 

We have seen that a similar condition may be stated in the case of 
the cosine series, and may evidently also be given for the sine series, in 
which the second integrated series takes the place of the first in this 
statement, the integral (first integral) being replaced by a second integral. 
his form of the condition has the advantage of only comparing sine series 
with sine series, and cosine series with cosine series.

Cor. 2.—If the points of non-convergence form a set which is at most 
of the first category im some interval, then the coefficients tend towards 
zero. 


For the points at which the excess of the upper function over the 
lower function is greater than 6 form, as is known, an inner limiting set, 
and therefore form in every interval in which they are dense everywhere 
a set of the second category, and cannot therefore be a sub-set of a set of 
the first category ; they must be nowhere dense therefore in an interval 
in which the points of non-uniform convergence form a set of the first | 
category. Thus the conditions of the corollary hold good, since this is 
true for all values of 6.

22. The considerations above exposed lead naturally, as in the case of 
harmonic series,* from the theory of non-harmonic Fourier series to that 
of restricted non-harmonic Fourier series. The definition is as follows : 


The p-th derived series of a non-harmonic Fourier cosine or sine series 
of F(x) is said to be a non-harmonic restricted Fourier cosine or sine 
series of the p-th class, and to be restricted to one or more intervals (a, 8), 
if, throughout each completely open interval in (a, 8), F(a) is a p-th in- 
tegral. The p-th differential coefficient of F(x) is then said to be the 
associated function of the restricted Fourier serves in any of these wnter- 
vals of restriction. 

Tf, in addition, the coefficients of the restricted Fourver serves tend to 
zero as we advance along the series, it is said to be “ordinary,” and is 
denoted briefly as a non-harmonic R. F. serves. 


We have already seen that a non-harmonic Fourier sine or cosine series 
satisfies both these conditions in the closed interval (0, 27). 
One of the fundamental properties of non-harmonic restricted Fourier 


* W.H. Young, ‘‘ On the Convergence of the Derived Series of Fourier Series ’’ (1916), 
Proc. London Math. Soc., Ser. 2, Vol. 17 (1919), pp. 195-236; ‘‘ On the Ordinary Conver- 
gence of Restricted Fourier Series’’ (1917), Proc. Roy. Soc., (A), Vol. 93, pp. 276-292; “On 
Restricted Fourier Series and the Convergence of Power Series’’ (1917), Proc. London Math. 
Soc., Ser. 2, Vol. 17 (1919), pp. 353-366. 


328 Pror. W. H. Youne [March 14, 


series* is embodied in the following theorems :— 

Tarorem.—The upper and lower functions at any point internal to 
the interval of restriction are independent of the nature of the associated 
function, except in an arbitrary small neighbourhood surrounding the 
point, provided the individual terms of the serves tend to zero at the point 
as we advance along the series ; indeed the part of the expression for the 
n-th partial summation depending on the remaining values of the func- 
tron tends uniformly to zero. 


Turorem.—In the case when the typical coefficuent itself converges to 
zero so that the series is “‘ ordinary”’, we can go still further and assert 
that the series behaves at any internal point of its interval of restriction 
precisely luke the Fourier series of a function equal to the associated 
function of the serves in the neighbourhood of the point and having any 
convenient values elsewhere. 

These theorems remain true at the end-points, provided the associated 
function satisfy at the point im question the appropriate “ continuation” 
formula applying to the point.

“‘ continuation ” formule, and in the meaning of the symbol ae. Taking, 
for example, the formula (A’) with the corresponding “ continuation ”’

A SIMPLE CONDITION FOR CO-APOLAR TRIANGLES 
By Pror. Wruuiam P. Minne. 


[Received November 7th, 191S.—Read November 14th, 1918.] 


Ir is known that, if ABC and DEF be any two triangles in the same 
plane, the locus of the point P moving so that the pencils P[ABC] and 
P[DEF] are apolar to one another is a cubic curve, which we call the 
Apolar Locus of ABC and DEF. The apolar locus is apolar to each of 
the triangles ABC and DEF regarded as degenerate class-cubics, and we 
eall such types of triangles “‘apolar triangles.” The converse problem 
may be stated as follows :—Given a cubic curve, to find two apolar tri- 
angles which subtend at every point on the curve pencils of lines apolar 
to one another. Such pairs of triangles are called ‘‘ co-apolar triangles.” 
In the Proc. London Math. Soc., Ser. 2, Vol. 17, p. 287, I found the 
necessary and sufficient condition for co-apolarity in a perfectly sym- 
metrical form by projecting the given pair of apolar triangles through a 
suitable point on the cubic into two new apolar triangles lying on the 
curve and having their six vertices on the same conic. By this means I 
was able to express the required condition in terms of the invariant pro- 
perties of two triads of points on a conic. The present solution is more 
direct and less complicated than the above. 

We shall deal with the reciprocal form of the above problem, because 
in the reciprocal form the geometry involved is more easy to visualize. 

Let the given class-cubic I be inscribed in the two triangles S, and 
S,, and let it be apolar to each of them. Let S, be chosen as the tri- 
angle of reference and let the three equations to S,, Sj, I’ be chosen as 
follows 


O,=ay7 — 0,

U, now, S a cubic curve represented by the general equation be taken 
and the apolar envelope of S and 9S, be formed, and if this apolar envelope 
be identified with I’, we shall find that the whole system of cubic-loci co- 
apolar with S,, with respect to I’, is:

We therefore have the following result: 


If two apolar triangles be co-apolar with respect to a class-cubte, the 
sides of erther triangle intersect the apolar locus of the other triangle and 
the class-cubic in nine points, through which passes a cubic curve having 
that other triangle as an inflexional triangle. 


We shall now prove the converse theorem, viz. 


The necessary and sufficient condition that two apolar triangles shall 
be co-apolar with respect to a class-cubic, is that the sides of either tri- 
angle must intersect the apolar locus of the other triangle and the class- 
cubic in nine points, through which passes a cubic curve having that other 
triangle as an inflexional triangle. 


To prove the above, let us form the general equation to the pencil of 
class-cubics inscribed in the triangles S,, S,, and apolar to them (7.e. satis- 
fying eight independent linear conditions). 

One such class-cubic is the apolar envelope of 8, and Sy, viz.
